Vikash Singh 1 Weicong Chen 1 Debargha Ganguly 1 Yanyan Zhang 1 Mohsen Hariri 1 Sreehari Sankar 1 Wang Yang 1 Chaoda Song 1 Alexander Nemecek 1 Osama Zafar 1 Yuting Shao 1 Kai Ye 1 Biyao Zhang 1 Xinpeng Li 1 Shouren Wang 1 Erman Ayday 1 Jing Ma 1 Sanmukh Rao Kuppannagari 1 Vipin Chaudhary 1 † † footnotetext: 1 Department of Computer and Data Sciences, Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH, USA.
Correspondence to: Vipin Chaudhary <vipin@case.edu>. Code: https://github.com/vicky157/grpo-validation . Abstract RLVR trains language models to reason by rewarding correct final answers. A widely used algorithm, group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), gives every token of a solution the same credit, so dense process rewards have been proposed to credit intermediate steps, for example with a Monte-Carlo reward that scores each step by sampling continuations from it. Comparing such a reward with GRPO per training update, or vetting it with a gradient test on a single batch, leaves out what it costs: in our setting, the continuations alone take 91 % 91\% of all generated tokens. We introduce a matched-compute protocol that gives every training intervention the same budget, measured in both generated tokens and training GPU-hours, and tests it in preregistered studies with controls for side effects. Across 1,668 1{,}668 runs on six models, we find that matching compute reverses the ranking. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, the Monte-Carlo reward leads standard GRPO per update, yet ends 14.3 14.3 percentage points lower at equal tokens and 7.8 7.8 points lower at equal training time, and none of four dense-reward variants outperforms GRPO even when given as much training time. A per-token gradient test orders these rewards as training does but overestimates each by one to two points, as much as the gains it is meant to detect. In contrast, training on the prompts whose predicted success is nearest 1 / 2 1/2 improves accuracy at matched compute, by 1.7 1.7 points on Qwen3-0.6B and 0.8 0.8 on Qwen2.5-1.5B. These gains replicate on fresh seeds and unseen problems, and a simple rule, that the candidate prompts are predicted to fail more often than not, predicted where they appear in five of six preregistered tests. The protocol also measures what such gains are worth: they come early in training, and standard GRPO with twice the initial learning rate comes within half a point of them. We argue that training interventions should be validated at matched compute, which overturns some apparent gains and confirms others. 1 Introduction Intervention How one training iteration assigns credit Generated tokens up to B Accuracy vs. outcome-only, at equal tokens; light: GPU-hours Outcome-only GRPO one advantage per completion same credit on every token rollouts 584 updates reference Monte-Carlo reward 4 continuations at each of 5 cuts credit per segment from continuation success 91% on continuations 50 updates −14.3 −7.8 ahead at equal updates Learned-critic reward ridge critic on hidden states critic no continuations; critic refit online rollouts 584 updates −0.4 to +0.7 −1.2 to −0.7 Prompt selection keep the 6 of 24 candidates closest to predicted success 1 2 \tfrac{1}{2} and 2 more at random critic scores candidates rollouts + one prefill pass to score candidates +0.8; +1.7 on Qwen3-0.6B +0.5; +1.0 on Qwen3-0.6B −15 −10 −5 0 points Figure 1: Counting every token changes which intervention wins. Four ways to train Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with GRPO on MATH, compared at a matched budget B B of tokens generated during training ( 12.25 12.25 M). Bars show accuracy minus outcome-only GRPO, to scale ( 5 5 seeds for the Monte-Carlo reward, 24 24 for the critics, and 60 60 for prompt selection, 54 54 on Qwen3-0.6B); light bars give the same comparisons at equal training GPU-hours on H200s (exploratory). Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a standard way to improve the reasoning of large language models ( Shao et al., 2024 ; DeepSeek-AI et al., 2025 ) . The model samples solutions to problems whose final answers a verifier can check, and training makes the correct solutions more likely. A widely used algorithm, group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), weights every token of a sampled solution, or completion, by the same advantage , the completion’s reward relative to the others sampled for the same prompt. A solution that sets up a problem correctly but slips in its last step is penalized for its good steps as well as its bad one. Dense rewards , also known as process rewards, give different steps different credit, derived from human step labels ( Lightman et al., 2024 ) , from a prover’s progress ( Setlur et al., 2025 ) , or from rewards learned on outcome labels ( Cui et al., 2026 ) . A step can also be scored by the probability of reaching the correct answer from it, estimated by sampling continuations from the partial solution, giving a Monte-Carlo reward ( Wang et al., 2024 ; Kazemnejad et al., 2025 ) , or predicted by a learned value model (a critic ). The hidden cost of dense rewards. How should we decide whether a dense reward helps? Two checks are cheap enough to run on any candidate reward, and a reward that passes both looks worth using. A gradient test takes a fixed batch of samples and asks whether the reward’s update points in the same direction as an independent estimate of the gradient of expected accuracy ( Section 2.4 ). A per-update comparison trains one model with the reward and one with outcome-only GRPO , the algorithm above, which credits only the final answer, and asks which is more accurate after the same number of updates. However, neither check considers what a dense reward costs. First, it consumes generated tokens. In our setting, the Monte-Carlo reward’s continuations account for 91 % 91\% of all generated tokens, so with the same tokens, outcome-only GRPO makes 584 584 updates and the Monte-Carlo reward 50 50 ( Figure 1 ). Second, it consumes GPU time, though not in proportion to its tokens. The continuations are only generated and scored, never trained on, and an inference engine generates tokens in large batches more cheaply than a training step processes them. With the same tokens, outcome-only GRPO therefore trains three times as long. A reward that wins per update can lose per token, and a comparison that counts only tokens can mislead about GPU-hours. Even without counting cost, the gradient test can mislead. In our probes, the Monte-Carlo reward’s update aligns closely with a reference gradient (mean cosine 0.84 0.84 ). Yet even with the same number of updates as outcome-only GRPO, adding it with a small weight is equivalent to outcome-only GRPO within ± 3 \pm 3 points of greedy accuracy, and adding it with a large weight loses 12.4 12.4 points ( Section 3.1 ). We therefore ask whether a dense reward that passes the gradient test still improves GRPO once its compute is counted, and if not, where the generated tokens should go instead. Our approach. We compare training interventions at matched compute : every method gets the same budget, measured on the same runs in generated tokens, including continuations and discarded samples, and in training GPU-hours, broken down by training stage. We apply this protocol to two interventions that spend compute in opposite ways. Dense rewards spend generated tokens to improve each update, whereas prompt selection spends one forward pass over candidate prompts to choose those whose predicted success is near 1 / 2 1/2 , since a group whose completions are all correct or all wrong produces no update. We reimplemented the GRPO trainer and ran 1,668 1{,}668 runs ( 1,468 1{,}468 GPU-hours) on six Qwen and Llama models. Because the gains we expected were small, comparable to the variation between random seeds, we preregistered each study, i.e., fixed its hypotheses, primary comparisons, and equivalence margins in a written plan before running it. Configurations compared within a study share prompts and evaluation samples under each seed, so each seed yields a paired difference. Controls separate an intervention’s intended effect from its side effects: random choice among the same candidate prompts (for a faster pass through the prompt stream), a baseline with a retuned learning rate (for an under-tuned baseline), and a strict verifier (for drift in what the reward accepts). Findings. Our metric is the accuracy of sampled solutions to held-out MATH problems (pass@1), and we report differences in percentage points. (1) Matching compute reverses the ranking. The Monte-Carlo reward leads outcome-only GRPO at equal updates but ends 14.3 14.3 points behind at equal tokens and 7.8 7.8 behind at equal training time, in every seed. Even given as much training time as outcome-only GRPO, none of the four dense-reward variants we tested shows a gain ( Figure 3 a). (2) Gradient tests rank but cannot certify. Per generated token, the efficiency test orders the Monte-Carlo variants as training does, yet it overestimates the accuracy reached with every reward we checked by one to two points, as large as the gains we measured. (3) Choosing which prompts to train on yields a gain that the protocol confirms and explains. Training on the candidate prompts whose predicted success is nearest 1 / 2 1/2 improves accuracy by 1.7 1.7 points on Qwen3-0.6B and 0.8 0.8 on Qwen2.5-1.5B at equal tokens, and by 1.0 1.0 and 0.5 0.5 at equal training time (exploratory). Both gains replicate on fresh seeds and on 1,543 1{,}543 unseen MATH test problems, and random choice among the same candidates does not reproduce them. The gain appears where the candidates are predicted to fail more often than not, a rule that held in five of six preregistered tests, and outcome-only GRPO with twice the initial learning rate comes within half a point of it. (4) A lenient verifier changes what training optimizes. With a verifier that also accepts answers outside \boxed{} , runs gradually stopped boxing their answers. A strict verifier removed this drift without lowering accuracy. Our contributions are a matched-compute protocol for evaluating RL training interventions, whose trainer, preregistrations, and run records we release ( Section 2 ), and two case studies in which the protocol rejects one intervention and confirms another ( Sections 3 and 4 ). We also characterize exactly when a dense reward’s update on a fixed batch equals some reweighting of whole completions ( Appendix I ), and we draw lessons for validating training interventions ( Section 6 ). 2 Background and Protocol 2.1 GRPO and Credit For a prompt x x , GRPO samples a group of G G completions (rollouts) from the policy π θ \pi_{\theta} , and a verifier gives each completion a reward R g ∈ { 0 , 1 } R_{g}\in\{0,1\} , which is 1 1 if its final answer is correct. Each completion’s advantage is its reward relative to the rest of the group: A g = R g − R ¯ std ⁡ ( R ) + ϵ , R ¯ = 1 G ​ ∑ g = 1 G R g , ϵ > 0 . A_{g}=\frac{R_{g}-\bar{R}}{\operatorname{std}(R)+\epsilon},\qquad\bar{R}=\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g=1}^{G}R_{g},\quad\epsilon>0. (1) A policy-gradient update multiplies the log-probability gradient of each sampled token by a scalar weight, the token’s credit c g , h c_{g,h} : u = ∑ g = 1 G ∑ h c g , h ​ s g , h , u=\sum_{g=1}^{G}\sum_{h}c_{g,h}\,s_{g,h}, (2) where s g , h = ∇ θ ​ log ​ π θ ​ ( a g , h ∣ x , a g , < h ) s_{g,h}=\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a_{g,h}\mid x,a_{g,<h}) is the log-probability gradient, or score , of the h h -th token a g , h a_{g,h} of completion g g . Outcome-only GRPO gives every token of a completion the same credit, c g , h = A g c_{g,h}=A_{g} . Its update, g out = ∑ g A g ​ q g g_{\mathrm{out}}=\sum_{g}A_{g}q_{g} , is therefore a weighted sum of the completions’ summed scores q g = ∑ h s g , h q_{g}=\sum_{h}s_{g,h} . We write V = span ⁡ { q 1 , … , q G } V=\operatorname{span}\{q_{1},\dots,q_{G}\} for the set of all such weighted sums, that is, of all updates that weight each completion as a whole. Any update in V V could also come from reweighting whole completions, so what a dense reward adds beyond such a reweighting lies outside V V ( Section 2.4 ). A group whose completions all succeed or all fail has A g = 0 A_{g}=0 for every g g and produces no update. A dense reward makes the credit vary within a completion. It adds a token-level term, c g , h = A g + w g , h c_{g,h}=A_{g}+w_{g,h} , that raises the credit of steps estimated to increase the chance of success and lowers the credit of steps estimated to decrease it. Its update can therefore leave V V . Ratio clipping, KL penalties, and the optimizer can transform u u . 2.2 Interventions and What They Cost We test two kinds of intervention ( Figure 1 , Table 2 ): dense rewards, which change the credit c g , h c_{g,h} , and prompt selection, which changes which prompts are sampled. Their costs fall on generated tokens, on training time, or on both. Section 3 also tests a learned critic and two variants that reuse samples, and Section 4 tests simpler selection rules; we describe each where we test it. Monte-Carlo reward. Following Wang et al. (2024) ; Kazemnejad et al. (2025) , we cut each completion at five fixed token positions, which splits it into six segments, and sample k k continuations from the prefix that ends at each cut. The fraction of continuations that end with a correct answer estimates the prefix’s value , i.e., the probability that it leads to a correct answer. Each segment’s credit is the change in estimated value from its start to its end. The continuations are only scored and then discarded, so this reward costs generated tokens: at k = 4 k=4 , it uses 91 % 91\% of all generated tokens. Prompt selection. Because uniform groups produce no update ( Section 2.1 ), several methods aim training at prompts whose success rate is near 1 / 2 1/2 ( Gao et al., 2026 ; Qu et al., 2026 ; Sun et al., 2025 ) . In each training iteration, our critic filter draws 24 24 candidate prompts and predicts each one’s success with a ridge critic on the policy’s hidden state at the end of the prompt. It trains on the 6 6 candidates whose predictions are nearest 1 / 2 1/2 , plus 2 2 others chosen at random. Scoring the candidates takes one forward (prefill) pass and generates no tokens. As a control, random choice trains on 8 8 of the same 24 24 candidates chosen at random. 2.3 Matched-Compute Protocol Models and recipe. Resolving small effects takes many paired seeds, so we train six small models: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct ( Qwen Team, 2024 ; Yang et al., 2024 ) with completions of up to 380 380 tokens, and Qwen3 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B in non-thinking mode ( Yang et al., 2025 ) and Llama-3.2-1B- and 3B-Instruct ( Grattafiori et al., 2024 ) with completions of up to 768 768 tokens. The training pool consists of MATH problems with numeric answers ( Hendrycks et al., 2021 ) , so that the verifier’s answer check is well defined. It uses levels 4–5 in the dense-reward studies and levels 3–5 in the prompt-selection studies, except those that test level 5 or levels 1–2 alone. Each training iteration uses 8 8 prompts with 8 8 completions each and takes one on-policy Adam step at learning rate 10 − 6 10^{-6} , without ratio clipping, sample reuse (except in the reuse variants), or a KL penalty. Two budgets. Generated tokens and training time can rank methods differently ( Section 1 ), so we set both. The token budget B B counts every token generated during training, including continuations and the groups that dynamic sampling discards; a run ends once it has generated B B tokens. A preregistered rule set B B for each model to the number of tokens that the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward generates in 48 48 iterations ( B = 12.25 B=12.25 M for Qwen2.5-1.5B; Section C.1 lists the others). Even with the most expensive reward, training thus makes about 50 50 updates. Training time , in GPU-hours, counts every training stage of a run: prompt scoring, generation, credit computation, the update, and the weight sync, but not evaluation or logging. To compare at equal training time, we compare the runs of one study, on one GPU type, at the time the faster configuration takes to reach B B . We also report every comparison at an equal number of updates, the comparison we argue against. Metric. We measure accuracy , the quantity that training and the gradient tests target: the fraction of 8 8 completions, sampled at temperature 1 1 , that the verifier marks correct (pass@1), averaged over 200 200 held-out numeric-answer MATH test problems. We take it from the first evaluation at or after the budget B B , without selecting checkpoints. We report differences in accuracy in percentage points ; figure axes show them as fractions ( 0.01 0.01 is one point). Preregistration and statistics. Before its runs, each study fixed in a plan file its hypotheses, its primary contrasts, its Holm families, and its equivalence margin ( ± 2 \pm 2 points, or ± 1 \pm 1 in four studies on Qwen2.5-1.5B). We report means with 95 % 95\% paired- t t intervals over seeds in brackets. We label each claim primary (preregistered, with p p -values adjusted for multiple comparisons by Holm’s method), secondary (preregistered, unadjusted), or exploratory (not preregistered, marked † ). To show that two configurations are equivalent within a margin, we use two one-sided tests (TOST). A crossed bootstrap over seeds and test problems checks every primary contrast. Tables 3 and 4 list every contrast, and Appendix C reports every secondary and exploratory analysis. Verifier. The verifier defines the reward, so a lenient one can change what training optimizes. The default verifier credits the boxed answer, or the last number in the completion when no answer is boxed; the strict verifier credits only boxed answers. Runs trained with the default verifier can drift away from boxing their answers, which can bias a comparison in either direction. The strict verifier gives no reward for unboxed answers and so rules out this drift; studies marked “strict” train with it. 2.4 Gradient Tests Gradient tests are a cheap way to vet a dense reward before training it ( Section 1 ), so we measure two and check in Section 3.3 whether they predict training. Both compare a method’s update, on fixed batches at one policy, with an estimate h ^ \widehat{h} of the gradient of expected accuracy from separately sampled continuations. Alignment is the cosine between the components of a dense update and of h ^ \widehat{h} that lie outside V V , the only part of a dense update that can improve, to first order, on reweighting whole completions. Efficiency , ( 𝔼 ⁡ ⟨ u , h ^ ⟩ ) 2 / 𝔼 ​ ‖ u ‖ 2 (\mathbb{E}\langle u,\widehat{h}\rangle)^{2}/\mathbb{E}\|u\|^{2} for an update direction u u , also accounts for how noisy an update is, and for a smooth objective it is proportional to the best expected gain of one SGD step along u u . We compute it over 96 96 fixed batches at the initial policy and report it relative to outcome-only GRPO, per update and per generated token; the per-token value divides by the tokens each update consumes. Appendix A gives further details of the recipe, the statistics, the verifier, and the gradient tests. 3 Case Study: Dense Rewards Of the interventions we test, dense rewards cost the most compute, and they are the ones that per-update comparisons and gradient tests favor. We examine them in three ways: per update, where they lead ( Section 3.1 ); per generated token and per GPU-hour, where they lose ( Section 3.2 ); and through the gradient efficiency test, which anticipates the reversal but overestimates every reward it rates ( Section 3.3 ). 3.1 Does a Dense Reward Help per Update? The gradient tests favor it. We start with a per-update comparison, an equal-iteration study. It compares configurations after the same number of iterations and uses its own recipe: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, fully fine-tuned at learning rate 10 − 5 10^{-5} for 32 32 iterations and scored by greedy validation accuracy ( Appendix G ). In that study, the Monte-Carlo reward is centered within each completion, normalized, and added with weight α \alpha : c g , h = A g + α ​ w g , h c_{g,h}=A_{g}+\alpha w_{g,h} . On four full-parameter probes with disjoint rollouts, the component of its update outside V V has mean cosine 0.84 0.84 with that of the reference gradient ( Appendix G ). To check that alignment is not automatic for any reward whose update leaves V V , we include two control rewards, whose updates lie outside V V to a similar extent (the part outside V V carries 0.86 0.86 – 0.95 0.95 of the update’s norm for all three rewards) but do not align with the reference. One is an implicit reward log ⁡ π θ − log ⁡ π ref \log\pi_{\theta}-\log\pi_{\mathrm{ref}} in the style of PRIME; the other shuffles the Monte-Carlo reward within each completion. Their cosines are − 0.01 ± 0.08 -0.01\pm 0.08 and − 0.01 ± 0.06 -0.01\pm 0.06 . In the matched-compute recipe, the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward is 1.56 × 1.56\times [ 1.28 , 2.03 ] [1.28,2.03] as efficient as outcome-only GRPO per update, and with the same number of updates it leads outcome-only GRPO by 1.5 1.5 [ 1.0 , 2.1 ] [1.0,2.1] points † ( Figure 2 a). Training does not follow, even at an equal number of iterations. In the equal-iteration study, outcome-only GRPO improves greedy accuracy by 5.6 5.6 [ 4.1 , 7.1 ] [4.1,7.1] points. In a prespecified expansion to 14 14 paired seeds, the Monte-Carlo reward with a low weight ( α = 0.25 \alpha=0.25 , 0.5 0.5 , or 1 1 ) is equivalent to outcome-only GRPO within ± 3 \pm 3 points (Holm-corrected bootstrap and paired- t t TOST), and with the high weight α = 5 \alpha=5 it loses 12.4 12.4 [ 8.7 , 16.1 ] [8.7,16.1] points ( n = 8 n=8 ). Other recipes point the same way. On Qwen2.5-7B, no dense-reward configuration in a 43 43 -run sweep exceeds the outcome baseline’s mean gain, SmolLM3-3B and a NuminaMath study show the same pattern, and only single-seed Phi-4 pilots are positive ( Section H.6 ). Alignment stays high even as training fails. In checkpoints taken every four iterations, the high-weight reward keeps an alignment of 0.969 0.969 to 1.000 1.000 with the reference on disjoint samples, while greedy accuracy falls from 25 % 25\% to 12 % 12\% and response length stays within 1 % 1\% of the low-weight runs. Entropy instead rises from 0.21 0.21 to 0.58 0.58 (low weight: 0.18 0.18 to 0.20 0.20 ), and the KL from the initial policy is about 30 30 times that of the low-weight runs. Because alignment does not decay, the loss is not explained by the reward pointing the wrong way; the rising entropy and KL are consistent with over-optimizing it ( Section H.5 ). Answer. The gradient tests say yes, and the Monte-Carlo reward leads at equal updates, but in the equal-iteration study it does not beat outcome-only GRPO. Figure 2: The gradient test ranks the Monte-Carlo reward first per update and last per generated token, the same order training follows at matched tokens; the critics’ higher per-token efficiency brings at most 0.7 0.7 points. Qwen2.5-1.5B on MATH L4–5. Horizontal: gradient efficiency relative to outcome-only GRPO ( Section 2.4 ), per update (a) or per generated token (b). Vertical: accuracy minus outcome-only GRPO on the same seeds at equal updates (a) or equal tokens (b). Red: the Monte-Carlo reward with k = 1 k=1 (triangle), 2 2 (square), and 4 4 (circle) continuations per cut ( 12 12 fresh seeds); green: the two critic forms ( 24 24 fresh seeds). In (a), outcome-only GRPO is interpolated to each reward’s update count (exploratory). Dashed line in (b): the heuristic extrapolation for a reward of that efficiency. Bars are 95 % 95\% CIs; the band marks ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 . 3.2 Does a Dense Reward Pay for Its Compute? Per generated token, the Monte-Carlo reward is far behind. At k = 4 k=4 , continuations take 91 % 91\% of the generated tokens, so by the time the token budget B B is used up, training with it has made 50 50 updates and outcome-only GRPO 584 584 . Its accuracy at B B is 14.3 14.3 [ 12.7 , 16.0 ] [12.7,16.0] points lower ( n = 5 n=5 , Holm p = 5 × 10 − 5 p=5\times 10^{-5} ), and 14.9 14.9 [ 14.0 , 15.8 ] [14.0,15.8] points lower on twelve fresh seeds ( Figure 2 b). The deficit recurs on Qwen3 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B ( 8.0 8.0 , 9.1 9.1 , and 10.0 10.0 points; n = 3 n=3 each), on GSM8K ( 9.8 9.8 ), and with 768 768 -token completions ( 6.6 6.6 , n = 8 n=8 ). Per GPU-hour, it is behind too. Not all tokens cost the same. With the same generated tokens, training takes 0.14 0.14 hours on an H200 with the k = 4 k=4 reward and 0.43 0.43 with outcome-only GRPO. Outcome-only GRPO spends 61 % 61\% of its time on updates, whereas the Monte-Carlo reward spends 73 % 73\% of its time generating continuations, which the inference engine generates in large batches ( Appendix D ). Even when it stops at the reward’s training time, outcome-only GRPO leads by 7.8 7.8 [ 6.8 , 8.8 ] [6.8,8.8] points, in all twelve seeds † . Cheaper rewards lose less. The randomness of continuation sampling contributes only 4 % 4\% of the second moment of the k = 4 k=4 update, so using fewer continuations should cut the cost while adding little noise. A preregistered study tests k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , and 4 4 on twelve fresh seeds. With one continuation per cut, which still spends 70 % 70\% of the budget on continuations, the reward trails outcome-only GRPO by 6.8 6.8 [ 6.0 , 7.6 ] [6.0,7.6] points and leads the k = 4 k=4 reward by 8.1 8.1 [ 7.1 , 9.0 ] [7.1,9.0] (both Holm p < 10 − 8 p<10^{-8} , in all twelve seeds); k = 2 k=2 falls in between ( − 11.2 -11.2 [ − 12.4 , − 10.1 ] [-12.4,-10.1] ). Compared at their own training times, the k = 2 k=2 and k = 1 k=1 rewards trail by 5.1 5.1 [ 4.0 , 6.2 ] [4.0,6.2] and 2.2 2.2 [ 1.7 , 2.8 ] [1.7,2.8] points, again in every seed † . Fewer continuations narrow the gap but do not close it. Reusing samples helps the reward but not the baseline. Another way to lower the cost is to get more out of each sample. A VinePPO-style variant ( Kazemnejad et al., 2025 ) uses value differences as advantages and, as in PPO, takes four clipped optimizer steps on each batch, and continuation reuse, adapted from SPO-tree and TreeRL ( Guo et al., 2025 ; Hou et al., 2025 ) , also trains on the continuations instead of discarding them ( Table 16 ). In three-seed pilots, this reuse raises the k = 4 k=4 reward’s accuracy at matched tokens by 7.7 7.7 points (Holm p = .007 p=.007 ) but lowers outcome-only GRPO’s by 5.9 5.9 ( Appendix E ). Even with as much training time as outcome-only GRPO, no variant shows a significant gain. The comparisons above stop when the faster configuration reaches B B . A preregistered study with 768 768 -token completions therefore trains four variants beyond T c T_{c} , the training time that outcome-only GRPO needs to reach B B ( 1.29 1.29 A100 GPU-hours; 8 8 seeds, strict verifier), and compares each with outcome-only GRPO at T c T_{c} (Holm over the four; Figure 3 a, Appendix F ). Two variants trail in every seed: the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward by 3.3 3.3 [ 2.4 , 4.3 ] [2.4,4.3] points (Holm p = 2 × 10 − 4 p=2\times 10^{-4} ), and continuation reuse by 5.2 5.2 [ 4.1 , 6.3 ] [4.1,6.3] ( p < 10 − 4 p<10^{-4} ), because its updates, which include the continuations, take 78 % 78\% of its training time ( 45 45 seconds per iteration, against 6 6 for outcome-only GRPO). The VinePPO-style variant trails by 1.1 1.1 [ 0.4 , 1.7 ] [0.4,1.7] ( p = .013 p=.013 ). This deficit lies within ± 2 \pm 2 points (TOST p = .005 p=.005 ), its crossed interval reaches zero, and at the variant’s own, shorter training time it is even with outcome-only GRPO ( + 0.3 +0.3 , secondary). The k = 1 k=1 reward’s deficit, 1.1 1.1 [ − 0.5 , + 2.8 ] [-0.5,+2.8] ( p = .15 p=.15 ), is neither significant nor shown to lie within ± 2 \pm 2 . Given still more time, the k = 1 k=1 reward reaches outcome-only GRPO’s accuracy at B B after 1.25 ​ T c 1.25\,T_{c} on the mean curves, and the other three do not within their runs, which end at 1.27 1.27 to 2.5 ​ T c 2.5\,T_{c} † ( Appendix F ). Answer. No. Per generated token, every Monte-Carlo variant loses 3 3 to 15 15 points. Given as much training time as outcome-only GRPO, none outperforms it, and the two closest trail by 1.1 1.1 points. Bands are 95 % 95\% CIs; the gray bands mark ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 . 3.3 Can a Gradient Test Predict the Result? Per generated token, the efficiency test anticipates the reversal. The k = 4 k=4 reward is 1.56 × 1.56\times as efficient as outcome-only GRPO per update but only 0.145 × 0.145\times [ 0.120 , 0.190 ] [0.120,0.190] per generated token. Per token, the test ranks the three Monte-Carlo rewards as k = 1 k=1 ( 0.45 × 0.45\times ), k = 2 k=2 ( 0.27 × 0.27\times ), and k = 4 k=4 ( 0.15 × 0.15\times ), the same order as training ( Figure 2 b). We had preregistered this order, together with the prediction that every Monte-Carlo reward would trail outcome-only GRPO, and both predictions held. Because the three are about equally efficient per update ( 1.55 1.55 to 1.63 × 1.63\times ), this order follows their token costs, and what the test adds is the size of each deficit. But it overestimates every reward it rates. Whether a reward is worth training depends on the size of its effect, not only its rank. To turn efficiency into a predicted accuracy, we use a heuristic extrapolation, not a derived prediction: we suppose that a reward with per-token efficiency e e reaches, after t t tokens, the accuracy that outcome-only GRPO reaches after e ​ t et tokens. This predicts 35.9 % 35.9\% , 31.9 % 31.9\% , and 27.5 % 27.5\% accuracy at B B for k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , and 4 4 ; the rewards actually reach 34.3 % 34.3\% , 29.9 % 29.9\% , and 26.3 % 26.3\% † . A learned critic tests the extrapolation where it is most favorable to dense rewards. Instead of sampling continuations, which dominate the cost of the Monte-Carlo reward, it predicts each prefix’s value with a ridge regression on the policy’s hidden states, refit each iteration on earlier rollouts; its forward passes and fits still add training time. Its temporal-difference (TD) form credits each segment with the change in predicted value, and its baseline form subtracts the predicted value from the final reward. Per token, the critic is 1.36 × 1.36\times [ 1.20 , 1.62 ] [1.20,1.62] as efficient as outcome-only GRPO in its TD form and 1.15 × 1.15\times [ 0.97 , 1.39 ] [0.97,1.39] in its baseline form. On 24 24 fresh Qwen2.5-1.5B seeds, both forms are equivalent to outcome-only GRPO within ± 2 \pm 2 points (TOST, Holm p < 10 − 4 p<10^{-4} ). The TD form changes accuracy by − 0.4 -0.4 [ − 1.2 , + 0.3 ] [-1.2,+0.3] points, and the baseline form ends 0.7 0.7 [ 0.2 , 1.2 ] [0.2,1.2] points ahead (Holm p = .016 p=.016 ), a lead that appears only at the final evaluation. Fitting the critic adds 23 23 to 24 % 24\% to training time, so at equal training time both forms trail outcome-only GRPO, by 1.2 1.2 [ 0.7 , 1.7 ] [0.7,1.7] and 0.7 0.7 [ 0.1 , 1.2 ] [0.1,1.2] points † . Where the extrapolation predicts average leads of 2.0 2.0 and 0.9 0.9 points, the critics lead by 0.2 0.2 and 0.0 0.0 , shortfalls we had preregistered ( p < 10 − 5 p<10^{-5} each). Across all five rewards, the extrapolation overestimates accuracy by 0.8 0.8 to 1.8 1.8 points on average, and every interval excludes zero (exploratory for the Monte-Carlo rewards). The test therefore cannot certify a gain of a point or two. It describes a single SGD step, with its step size tuned to each update, at the initial policy, whereas training takes many Adam steps at a fixed learning rate as the policy changes. Answer. It predicts the order, not the size. The test ranks the Monte-Carlo rewards as training does but overestimates all five rewards by 0.8 0.8 to 1.8 1.8 points, as large as the gains we measured. 4 Case Study: Prompt Selection Dense rewards show what the protocol rejects; prompt selection shows what it confirms. Rather than spending generated tokens to improve the update on prompts already sampled, prompt selection spends one forward pass to decide which prompts to sample ( Section 2.2 ). The protocol confirms its gain, shows where the gain comes from ( Section 4.1 ), predicts where it appears ( Section 4.2 ), and measures what it is worth ( Section 4.3 ). 4.1 Does Prompt Selection Help? It gains on two of six models with the level 3–5 pool, and both gains replicate. On Qwen3-0.6B, the critic filter raises accuracy by 1.7 1.7 [ 1.2 , 2.1 ] [1.2,2.1] points over 54 54 seeds from four studies (and is higher in 42 42 of them), including a gain of 1.2 1.2 [ 0.5 , 1.9 ] [0.5,1.9] points in a preregistered replication on 24 24 fresh seeds (Holm p = .003 p=.003 ). On Qwen2.5-1.5B, the gain is smaller but holds in three preregistered studies ( 12 12 , 24 24 , and 24 24 seeds; Holm p = .039 p=.039 , .006 .006 , and .034 .034 ); over all 60 60 seeds, it is 0.8 0.8 [ 0.4 , 1.1 ] [0.4,1.1] points. The other four models show no significant gain on this pool ( Figure 4 a), and Section 4.2 explains where the filter helps. It carries over to problems that no study had evaluated on. Every study evaluated on the same 200 200 test problems, and on them the crossed bootstrap interval for the Qwen2.5-1.5B gain reached zero each time, so we tested transfer directly. The last study on each model also scored, at B B , the 1,543 1{,}543 numeric-answer MATH test problems of levels 3–5 that lie outside all evaluation sets, and the 1,319 1{,}319 GSM8K test problems. On these MATH problems, the filter gains 0.8 0.8 [ 0.5 , 1.1 ] [0.5,1.1] points on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 1.1 1.1 [ 0.6 , 1.5 ] [0.6,1.5] on Qwen3-0.6B (Holm p < 10 − 4 p<10^{-4} each), with crossed intervals excluding zero. On GSM8K, which neither configuration trains on, it gains 1.1 1.1 and 1.3 1.3 points (secondary). It comes from which prompts are chosen. Because it draws 24 24 candidates per iteration and trains on 8 8 , the filter also moves through the prompt stream three times as fast as outcome-only GRPO, which could help on its own. Random choice among the same candidates isolates this side effect. In preregistered primary contrasts, random choice trails the filter on both models where the filter helps: by 1.7 1.7 [ 0.5 , 2.9 ] [0.5,2.9] points on Qwen3-0.6B ( p = .008 p=.008 , twelve fresh seeds), and on Qwen2.5-1.5B by 1.0 1.0 [ 0.4 , 1.6 ] [0.4,1.6] (Holm p = .011 p=.011 ) and again by 0.9 0.9 [ 0.3 , 1.5 ] [0.3,1.5] on 24 24 fresh seeds (Holm p = .007 p=.007 ). Random choice itself is equivalent to outcome-only GRPO within ± 2 \pm 2 points on all three models tested. Most of it survives when training time is counted. Scoring the candidates costs a forward pass, so the filter’s runs take 9 9 to 14 % 14\% longer to reach B B . At equal training time, the filter still leads, by 1.0 1.0 [ 0.6 , 1.5 ] [0.6,1.5] points on Qwen3-0.6B and 0.5 0.5 [ 0.2 , 0.8 ] [0.2,0.8] on Qwen2.5-1.5B † ( Figure 3 b); on the seeds of the Qwen3-0.6B replication alone, this lead is 0.4 0.4 [ − 0.1 , + 1.0 ] [-0.1,+1.0] . A per-prompt success estimate carries the gain, and cheaper signals do not. Simpler rules test which part of the filter carries the gain. A history filter, close to GRESO ( Zheng et al., 2025 ) , uses the reward of each prompt’s most recent group and needs no forward pass. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, it gains 0.6 0.6 [ 0.1 , 1.1 ] [0.1,1.1] points (Holm p = .035 p=.035 , 36 36 seeds), also at equal training time, while the learned critic adds an early lead of 1.9 1.9 [ 1.6 , 2.1 ] [1.6,2.1] points over it during the first half of the budget (secondary). On Qwen3-0.6B and 1.7B, filtering by MATH difficulty level gains nothing ( + 0.2 +0.2 , TOST p = .005 p=.005 ), and DAPO’s dynamic sampling ( Yu et al., 2025 ) loses 1.8 1.8 [ 0.8 , 2.7 ] [0.8,2.7] points, because the groups it discards after generating them count toward the budget. Answer. Yes, on two of six models: by 1.7 1.7 and 0.8 0.8 points at equal tokens and by 1.0 1.0 and 0.5 0.5 at equal training time (exploratory). The gains replicate on fresh seeds and unseen problems and come from the choice of prompts. Figure 4: Where prompt selection helps is predicted by how hard its candidates are, not by how many informative groups it adds. (c) The critic’s mean predicted success of the candidates against the change in accuracy; the dashed line marks 1 / 2 1/2 . Bars are 95 % 95\% CIs over seeds; the gray band marks ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 . 4.2 When Does Prompt Selection Help? Not where it adds the most informative groups. The rationale for training on prompts near 1 / 2 1/2 , and for dynamic sampling ( Gao et al., 2026 ; Yu et al., 2025 ) , is that only mixed-outcome groups have nonzero advantages. The filter raises the share of these groups by 8 8 to 19 19 points in every setting at learning rate 10 − 6 10^{-6} . Accuracy, however, rises in only some settings, and not in those where the share rises most ( Figure 4 b), so this rationale does not predict it. It helps where the candidates are predicted to fail more often than not. We formed this account post hoc from five settings. In them, the critic’s mean predicted success over the 24 24 candidates separates the two settings where the filter helped ( 0.42 0.42 and 0.40 0.40 ) from the rest ( 0.54 0.54 to 0.70 0.70 ), with no overlap between seeds, and the predictions are calibrated ( Table 9 ). Random choice, which trains on a uniform draw of the candidates, realizes the predicted success to within 0.2 0.2 points on all three models. The rule follows from how the filter chooses prompts. When the mean prediction is below 1 / 2 1/2 , keeping the candidates nearest 1 / 2 1/2 shifts training toward easier prompts and away from groups that are all wrong; when it is above 1 / 2 1/2 , the filter shifts training toward harder prompts. Outcome-only GRPO’s own training reward separates the first eleven settings in the same way ( Table 8 ), so a short pilot run is enough to apply the rule. Five of six preregistered tests agree with it ( Table 1 ). The two most direct tests hold the model and pool fixed and change only the filter’s target, to predicted success 0.25 0.25 , so that the filter moves training toward prompts harder than its candidates: on both models where the gain had replicated, it disappears. Two tests change the training pool: the filter helps on Qwen3-1.7B with level 5 (mean prediction 0.39 0.39 ), although its crossed interval reaches zero, and not on Qwen3-0.6B with levels 1–2 ( 0.79 0.79 ). Two change the model family: it does not help on Llama-3.2-3B ( 0.68 0.68 ), as predicted, nor on Llama-3.2-1B ( 0.42 0.42 ), the one failed prediction. Because four of the five confirmed predictions are of no gain, the more common outcome, the two direct manipulations weigh most ( Appendix B ). Answer. Where candidates are predicted to fail more often than not. The filter helps significantly in four of the five such settings and in none of the seven others ( Figure 4 c); on a fixed model and pool, aiming it at harder prompts removes the gain. Bands are 95 % 95\% CIs; the gray band marks ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 . 4.3 What Is the Gain Worth? It is a head start that saves about a fifth of the token budget. The lead forms early: on Qwen2.5-1.5B, it peaks at 2.6 2.6 [ 2.2 , 3.0 ] [2.2,3.0] points at three-sixteenths of the budget ( 3 ​ B / 16 3B/16 ) and falls to 0.8 0.8 at B B ( Figure 5 ). On both models, the filter reaches outcome-only GRPO’s final accuracy with 0.78 0.78 of the token budget, and with 0.85 0.85 and 0.87 0.87 of its training time, including the time spent scoring candidates † . On Qwen3-0.6B, the gain also holds at pass@8, by 0.9 0.9 [ 0.2 , 1.7 ] [0.2,1.7] points, and at every test level, whereas on Qwen2.5-1.5B it shrinks as k k grows † ( Table 10 ). With longer training, outcome-only GRPO catches up. A preregistered study trained both Qwen3-0.6B configurations to twice the budget (strict verifier, 24 24 seeds): the filter leads by 1.9 1.9 [ 1.3 , 2.6 ] [1.3,2.6] points at B B but by only 0.5 0.5 [ − 0.5 , + 1.6 ] [-0.5,+1.6] at 2 ​ B 2B , which is equivalent to zero within ± 2 \pm 2 points (TOST p = .004 p=.004 ). A retuned baseline gains about as much, so the baseline sets the benchmark. A head start could also reflect a baseline that learns too slowly, so we retuned its learning rate. On Qwen3-0.6B, a preregistered study (strict verifier, 24 24 seeds) doubled the constant learning rate of 10 − 6 10^{-6} , which raised outcome-only GRPO’s accuracy by 1.9 1.9 [ 0.5 , 3.3 ] [0.5,3.3] points (Holm p = .018 p=.018 ); the faster baseline minus the filter is − 0.03 -0.03 [ − 1.3 , + 1.2 ] [-1.3,+1.2] points, equivalent within ± 2 \pm 2 (TOST p = .002 p=.002 ; Figure 6 a). On Qwen2.5-1.5B, a doubled rate lowers both by about three points, and the filter’s gain holds ( 1.1 1.1 , Holm p = .018 p=.018 ). A rate that decays linearly from 2 × 10 − 6 2\times 10^{-6} to 0 0 at B B keeps the faster early rise without that loss ( Figure 6 b). With it, outcome-only GRPO ends 0.5 0.5 [ − 0.3 , + 1.2 ] [-0.3,+1.2] points below the filter at the constant rate (Holm p = .41 p=.41 ; equivalent within ± 2 \pm 2 but not shown within ± 1 \pm 1 ), and the filter’s own gain under the schedule, 0.2 0.2 [ − 0.5 , + 0.9 ] [-0.5,+0.9] , is equivalent to zero within ± 1 \pm 1 point (TOST p = .015 p=.015 ; Figure 6 c). Answer. A head start worth about a fifth of the budget. It fades by 2 ​ B 2B , and a retuned baseline comes within half a point of it. 5 Related Work The structure of policy-gradient updates. Writing an outcome update as a weighted sum of the completions’ summed scores follows from the score-function estimator ( Williams, 1992 ; Sutton et al., 1999 ) , and it applies to the completion-level advantages of GRPO and RLOO ( Shao et al., 2024 ; Ahmadian et al., 2024 ) ; Mishra et al. (2026) study this factorization for GRPO. Analyses of GRPO’s dynamics in expectation ( Mroueh, 2026 ) and of which credit schemes give proportional expected gradients ( Fu et al., 2026 ) leave open whether the updates computed on a finite batch are equal, which Appendix I answers exactly. The low-dimensional gradient subspaces measured by Schneider et al. (2024) also differ from the span V V of one batch’s completion scores. HAPO and EAPO redistribute advantages within completions while preserving their signs ( He et al., 2026 ; Yeo et al., 2026 ) , so their updates, too, can leave V V . The sampled gradients of potential-based shaping ( Ng et al., 1999 ) need not lie in V V either, and work on reward hacking ( Skalse et al., 2022 ; Gao et al., 2023 ) shows that optimizing a proxy reward too hard can lower the true objective. Process rewards and how they are combined with outcomes. Step-level supervision ( Lightman et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2024 ) , progress-based advantages ( Setlur et al., 2025 ) , Monte-Carlo value estimates ( Kazemnejad et al., 2025 ) , and implicit reward models trained online from outcome labels ( Yuan et al., 2025 ; Cui et al., 2026 ) report gains from feedback on intermediate steps. VinePPO reaches PPO’s peak accuracy in up to 3 × 3\times less wall-clock time and outperforms GRPO and RLOO, with 9 9 rollouts at every reasoning step, models fine-tuned on the task, and KL-regularized PPO steps; our adaptation keeps its value-difference advantage and clipped reuse but not its recipe ( Table 16 ). SPO-tree ( Guo et al., 2025 ) and TreeRL ( Hou et al., 2025 ) also train on the sampled continuations, so that their tokens are not spent on value estimates alone. How advantages are normalized, the level at which credit is assigned, and how uniform groups are handled affect how process and outcome rewards combine ( Wang et al., 2026 ; Agrawal et al., 2026 ) . Choosing training prompts. Because a uniform group gives GRPO no advantage, several methods aim training at prompts with success near 1 / 2 1/2 . Some measure success with rollouts first: DAPO discards uniform groups after generating them ( Yu et al., 2025 ) , online difficulty filtering keeps prompts whose pass rates fall in a band around 1 / 2 1/2 ( Bae et al., 2026 ) , and LILO ranks prompts by learnability ( Foster et al., 2025 ) . Others decide before generating: GRESO skips prompts whose recent groups were uniform ( Zheng et al., 2025 ) , MoPPS keeps a Bayesian estimate of each prompt’s success ( Qu et al., 2026 ) , DOTS predicts difficulty from similar questions ( Sun et al., 2025 ) , and PCL trains on the prompts whose value-model prediction is nearest 1 / 2 1/2 ( Gao et al., 2026 ) . Our critic filter uses PCL’s rule and our history filter resembles GRESO and MoPPS; rather than propose a new rule, we test such rules at matched compute. 6 Lessons and Conclusion Each lesson comes from a result that a cheaper protocol would have missed. (1) Count every generated token and every GPU-hour, and report updates alongside them. The Monte-Carlo reward ranked first at equal updates and last at equal tokens, and a critic’s small gain at equal tokens disappeared at equal training time. (2) Treat gradient tests as heuristics, not certificates. The efficiency test overestimated every reward it rated by one to two points, as large as the gains we measured. (3) Control for side effects. Random choice among the same candidates separated which prompts were chosen from how fast training moved through the prompt stream. (4) Test the mechanism, not only the method. The share of informative groups did not predict where prompt selection helped; the candidates’ predicted success did, in five of six preregistered tests. (5) Retune the baseline. Doubling the initial learning rate brought outcome-only GRPO within half a point of the critic filter on both models where its gain replicated. (6) Fix the verifier first. A lenient verifier let runs in either configuration drift away from boxing their answers. (7) Replicate on fresh seeds and on more than one model, and preregister. A six-seed gain on Qwen3-1.7B did not replicate on eighteen fresh seeds, and the one gain we predicted outside the Qwen family did not appear. Appendix B discusses the scope of these lessons and the limitations of our studies. Dense rewards can produce update directions that no reweighting of whole completions can, but only training at matched compute can show whether they help. None of our dense-reward variants beat outcome-only GRPO by more than a point, and the Monte-Carlo reward fell 14.3 14.3 points behind at equal tokens, while choosing which prompts to train on beat it by up to 1.7 1.7 points. Because that gain comes early, a faster-learning baseline nearly matches it: outcome-only GRPO with twice the initial learning rate comes within half a point. A claim that a training intervention helps should state its token and GPU-hour budgets, its verifier, the models on which it replicated, and how its baseline was tuned. References Agrawal et al. AI Usage Disclosure We used generative AI tools for grammar check, spelling mistakes and help us to visualize our results efficiently as well as debugging help during codebase development. Supplementary Material Reproducibility and Responsible Reporting The supplementary material gives the proofs, training and evaluation settings, seed counts, and statistical methods. The artifact at https://github.com/vicky157/grpo-validation contains the trainer and analysis code, every configuration and preregistration file with its dated amendments, each study’s report and submission record, and every run’s configuration, evaluation log (with training time), seconds per training stage, and compute record, from which every contrast at matched tokens or matched training time can be recomputed; one script rebuilds the training-time tables and figures. The per-iteration logs are omitted for size. We use public mathematical-reasoning benchmarks and released language-model checkpoints. The study involves no human participants or new personal-data collection. We report accuracy losses and inconclusive comparisons alongside positive results. Conventions. The appendix uses the terms defined in Section 2 , and reports accuracy and differences in accuracy as fractions, as the figures do ( 0.01 0.01 is one percentage point of the main text). Appendix A Additional Background This section gives the details of the protocol and of the gradient tests that Section 2 summarizes. Recipe. A preregistered stability rule set the learning rate to 10 − 6 10^{-6} , after 10 − 5 10^{-5} degraded every configuration over the matched horizon. The completion limit matters: at 380 380 tokens, 73 % 73\% of the initial samples are cut off, so part of what every run learns is to finish within the limit; at 768 768 tokens, 20 % 20\% are. Accuracy is averaged over held-out test problems of levels 3–5, or of the training pool’s own levels for the level-5 and level 1–2 pools. Both reuse variants keep the published form of credit but not the published step boundaries, sample counts, or recipes ( Table 16 ). Statistics. A study’s Holm families are the groups of tests corrected together. The plan files are internal rather than in a public registry; Appendix C audits their timing. Because every study evaluates on the same 200 200 test problems, a crossed bootstrap, which resamples seeds and test problems together, checks every primary contrast. Verifier drift. Some runs trained with the default verifier gradually stop boxing their answers, which shows up as accuracy under the default verifier exceeding that under the strict one by more than 10 10 points. This drift occurred in up to 49 49 of a study’s 408 408 evaluations and in either configuration. Preregistered studies that retrained the drifting configurations with the strict verifier found no drift ( 0 0 of 2,196 2{,}196 evaluations) and no loss in accuracy ( Section C.7 ). Alignment. Let J ⁡ ( θ ) J(\theta) be the expected accuracy of π θ \pi_{\theta} , h = ∇ J ​ ( θ ) h=\nabla J(\theta) its gradient, and h ^ \widehat{h} an estimate of h h from separately sampled continuations. Every update that weights whole completions lies in V V . A dense update d d can therefore improve on its projection P V ​ d P_{V}d onto V V , to first order, only through its component outside V V : ⟨ h , d − P V ​ d ⟩ = ⟨ P ⟂ ​ h , P ⟂ ​ d ⟩ , P ⟂ = I − P V \langle h,d-P_{V}d\rangle=\langle P_{\!\perp}h,P_{\!\perp}d\rangle,\qquad P_{\!\perp}=I-P_{V} (3) ( Proposition 1 ; Appendix I characterizes exactly which dense rewards produce updates outside V V ). The alignment test reports the cosine between P ⟂ ​ d P_{\!\perp}d and P ⟂ ​ h ^ P_{\!\perp}\widehat{h} , the components of the dense update and of the estimated gradient that lie outside V V . Efficiency. Alignment ignores how noisy an update is and what it costs; efficiency accounts for both. For an objective with smoothness constant L L , the gradient efficiency ( 𝔼 ⁡ ⟨ u , h ^ ⟩ ) 2 / 𝔼 ​ ‖ u ‖ 2 (\mathbb{E}\langle u,\widehat{h}\rangle)^{2}/\mathbb{E}\|u\|^{2} of an update direction u u equals 2 ​ L 2L times the best expected one-step gain of an SGD step along u u , max η ⁡ η ​ 𝔼 ​ ⟨ u , h ^ ⟩ − L 2 ​ η 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ ‖ u ‖ 2 \max_{\eta}\,\eta\mathbb{E}\langle u,\widehat{h}\rangle-\tfrac{L}{2}\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}\|u\|^{2} ; the expected inner product 𝔼 ​ ⟨ u , h ^ ⟩ \mathbb{E}\langle u,\widehat{h}\rangle was positive for every method we measured ( Appendix C ). Appendix B Scope and Open Validation Questions This section expands on the limitations in Section 6 . It first covers the matched-compute studies of Sections 3 and 4 , and then the equal-iteration study of Section 3.1 and the fixed-batch theory of Appendix I . B.1 Matched-compute studies Scope. These studies use an independent reimplementation of the trainer and a learning rate of 10 − 6 10^{-6} , rather than the equal-iteration study’s 10 − 5 10^{-5} , because the faster rate degraded every configuration over the matched horizon. They cover numeric-answer MATH problems, evaluation sets of 200 200 problems (on Qwen2.5-1.5B and Qwen3-0.6B, also 1,543 1{,}543 further MATH test problems and the GSM8K test set), models of up to 4 4 B parameters, and budgets of 8 8 – 35 35 M generated tokens. Most contrasts pool 6 6 – 60 60 seed pairs, but some, including primary contrasts of the first studies, have only n = 3 n=3 – 5 5 . Four of the six models are from the Qwen family, in which RLVR can behave differently from other families ( Shao et al., 2026 ) . We tested the other two, Llama-3.2-1B and 3B, only with prompt selection and on one pool. On 3B, the candidates’ mean predicted success was above 1 / 2 1/2 . On 1B it was below 1 / 2 1/2 , but the predicted gain did not appear, so we have shown no gain outside the Qwen family. Learning rate. Most runs use a constant learning rate of 10 − 6 10^{-6} . Three studies double it, and one decays it linearly from 2 × 10 − 6 2\times 10^{-6} to zero over the budget; we did not tune it for each model or configuration. On Qwen3-0.6B, doubling the rate gave outcome-only GRPO as large a gain as the filter, also without drift, so a baseline tuned for speed may leave less for prompt selection to add. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, doubling the rate lowered both configurations at the budget, and the filter’s gain held. With the decaying rate, however, outcome-only GRPO ended 0.005 0.005 below the filter at 10 − 6 10^{-6} , a difference that is neither significant nor shown to lie within ± 0.01 \pm 0.01 , and the filter’s gain under that schedule was equivalent to zero within ± 0.01 \pm 0.01 , a margin wider than the gain at the constant rate. We tried no warmup, no other peak rates, and no other schedules, and none of the schedules we tried was tuned for the filter. A schedule tuned for the filter might restore its gain, and one tuned for the baseline might close the remaining half point. Where prompt selection helps. Prompt selection helped Qwen3-0.6B (on levels 3–5 and 4–5), Qwen2.5-1.5B on levels 3–5, and Qwen3-1.7B on level 5. It did not help Qwen3-1.7B on levels 3–5, Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-0.6B on levels 1–2, or Llama-3.2-3B, and it did not help Llama-3.2-1B significantly. We formed the account that it helps when the candidates’ mean predicted success is below 1 / 2 1/2 post hoc, from five settings. Tests of the account. Two preregistered studies agreed with the account, one in each direction: they found a small gain ( + 0.011 +0.011 ) whose crossed interval reaches zero, and no gain where the candidates averaged 0.79 0.79 . Both moved the candidates across 1 / 2 1/2 by changing the training pool, which also changes the test set, so they do not separate the candidates’ difficulty from other properties of a pool. A third study kept the pool and changed the model. It agreed with the prediction of no gain, but the candidates fell on the other side of 1 / 2 1/2 from the one its design anticipated. Two of these three predictions were of no gain, which is the more common outcome here (seven of twelve settings), so they put the account at less risk than a predicted gain does. A fourth study tested a predicted gain on the smaller Llama-3.2-1B with the level 3–5 pool, chosen by a pilot because outcome-only GRPO’s training reward on it was below 0.45 0.45 . That prediction failed ( + 0.004 +0.004 [ − 0.001 , + 0.010 ] [-0.001,+0.010] , p = .095 p=.095 , equivalent to zero within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 ), although the filter’s mean lead was positive at every evaluation after the start. Of the account’s two predicted gains, therefore, one was found. A fifth study kept the model and pool fixed (Qwen3-0.6B on levels 3–5) and changed only the filter’s target, to 0.25 0.25 . As predicted, the filter then trained on prompts harder than its candidates and lost its gain. A sixth study repeated this on Qwen2.5-1.5B with the same pool and found the same result, although the deficit there ( − 0.010 -0.010 , as large as the gain with target 1 / 2 1/2 ) has a crossed interval that just reaches zero, and the filter kept a small head start that its preregistration did not predict. These two studies separate the direction in which the filter moves training from the pool, but only on one pool and on the two models where the filter gained. Applying the account. Both of the account’s predictors come from training runs. The base model’s accuracy on its first training prompts, which is known before training, does not separate the settings ( Table 8 ), so applying the account requires at least a short pilot run of outcome-only GRPO. On Llama-3.2-3B, a training reward far above held-out accuracy may reflect exposure to MATH training problems during pretraining; we did not test for contamination. The first studies, on a level 4–5 pool, were not used to form the account. They agree with it on Qwen3-0.6B and 4B ( 6 6 and 8 8 seeds) and are inconclusive on Qwen3-1.7B, whose candidates averaged 0.52 0.52 . Starting accuracy alone does not predict a gain: training on level 5 gives Qwen3-4B the starting accuracy of 0.6B, but no gain. The composition of the trained groups does not predict a gain either. Compared methods. Prior work also uses the selection rule itself. We compared it at matched tokens with dynamic sampling, the history filter, the level filter, and random choice, but not with methods that rank prompts by first sampling rollouts for them, such as LILO or online difficulty filtering ( Foster et al., 2025 ; Bae et al., 2026 ) . For dense rewards, we tested at matched tokens Monte-Carlo rewards with one, two, and four continuations at each of five cuts, whose continuations are scored and discarded; a ridge critic; and, with 768 768 -token completions, a variant that also trains on its continuations and a VinePPO-style advantage with rollout reuse. These variants adapt published methods to our trainer and recipe rather than reproduce them ( Table 16 ). VinePPO estimates values at every reasoning step with nine rollouts under a KL-regularized PPO recipe; SPO-tree trains on a three-level tree with a probability mask; and TreeRL branches at uncertain tokens and trains on every path from the root to a leaf. Faithful reproductions, fewer cuts, other tree-structured estimators, or a separately trained implicit reward model may trade off cost differently. Gradient test. The per-token gradient test ranked the three Monte-Carlo rewards in the same order as training, but only on one model. When we extrapolated it to training as a heuristic, it overestimated all five rewards we checked by about 0.01 0.01 to 0.02 0.02 (exploratory for the Monte-Carlo rewards and preregistered for the two critics; every interval excludes zero). Duration and transfer of the gain. We trained beyond the budget only on Qwen3-0.6B, where most of the gain was gone by 2 ​ B 2B ( + 0.005 +0.005 [ − 0.005 , + 0.016 ] [-0.005,+0.016] , compared with + 0.019 +0.019 at B B ). On Qwen2.5-1.5B, the gain replicated twice on fresh seeds, but in all three studies the crossed seed-by-problem bootstrap intervals on the 200 200 problems include zero. The third study therefore also scored the 1,543 1{,}543 MATH test problems of levels 3–5 that lie outside every other evaluation set. There, the crossed interval excludes zero ( + 0.008 +0.008 [ 0.004 , 0.012 ] [0.004,0.012] ), so the gain carries over to other problems from the same distribution. On GSM8K, an easier task that neither configuration trains on, the gain was + 0.011 +0.011 [ 0.006 , 0.016 ] [0.006,0.016] (secondary); we tested no other task. Measured with the strict verifier, both contrasts are unresolved, because one filter run (two on GSM8K) had drifted at B B ; without the drifted runs, both are positive (exploratory). On Qwen3-0.6B, a fourth study on fresh seeds replicated the gain on the 200 200 problems, at + 0.012 +0.012 compared with the first studies’ + 0.021 +0.021 , and found it on the further MATH problems (crossed interval [ 0.006 , 0.016 ] [0.006,0.016] ) and on GSM8K ( + 0.013 +0.013 ). At equal training time, however, its lead on those seeds alone was not significant ( + 0.004 +0.004 [ − 0.001 , + 0.010 ] [-0.001,+0.010] ). Multiplicity. Holm’s correction applies within each study, not across studies. Under a correction across studies, the replicated gains (Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen2.5-1.5B on levels 3–5) remain stronger evidence than single-study results with p p between .01 .01 and .05 .05 , such as the level-5 gain on Qwen3-1.7B, the history filter’s gain, and the baseline-form critic’s gain at the budget. Truncation. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, with a 380 380 -token limit, 73 % 73\% of the initial samples are cut off, so part of what every run learns there is to finish within the limit. The Monte-Carlo reward’s deficit is smaller with 768 768 -token completions and a budget calibrated for them ( − 0.061 -0.061 on three seeds and − 0.066 -0.066 on eight, compared with − 0.143 -0.143 ). With 768 768 -token completions and as much training time as outcome-only GRPO, the k = 4 k=4 reward and continuation reuse still trailed ( − 0.033 -0.033 and − 0.052 -0.052 ), and the VinePPO-style variant trailed within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 ( − 0.011 -0.011 ). The k = 1 k=1 reward’s deficit ( − 0.011 -0.011 [ − 0.028 , + 0.005 ] [-0.028,+0.005] ), however, was neither significant nor shown to lie within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 . We therefore claim neither that a cheap Monte-Carlo reward loses per GPU-hour in that setting nor that it matches outcome-only GRPO. Hardware and accounting. Every equal-time contrast compares runs on one GPU type. On other hardware, the ratio of generation time to update time may differ, and the equal-time results with it. Behavior at larger scales and on other tasks remains open. The token budget counts generated tokens. It does not count the prefill pass of the prompt critic, which we report as training time instead. Measured in training time, the head start in the three settings with a gain shrinks from 22 22 – 23 % 23\% to 13 13 – 17 % 17\% ( Table 11 ). B.2 Equal-iteration study and theory Sampled geometry. The score map describes one policy and one sampled batch, and the scores and the span of whole-completion updates change during training. Also, V V contains every reweighting of whole completions, a larger set than the one coefficient vector that a single outcome update uses. The geometry describes dense rewards used as training coefficients. Using process rewards to rank or search among solutions at inference time is a separate use, which it does not cover. Optimizer and controls. The neural runs use Adam with gradient-norm clipping at 1.0 1.0 . Per-coordinate scaling and optimizer state can change the direction of a raw gradient, so the geometric decomposition describes the gradient before these transformations. Permuting token weights preserves their magnitudes, but the norm of the projected gradient, the norm of the total update, and the policy KL can still change. To separate the effect of coherent credit from the effect of update size, controls must therefore be matched at the stage of the update being studied. Reference and evaluation target. The progress reference relies on a finite number of Monte-Carlo continuations, a segmentation of each prefix, a finite continuation horizon, and a verifier. Splitting samples avoids reusing them directly, but the reference keeps these estimator choices. The strongest check, with disjoint samples and full-parameter gradients, uses four Qwen2.5-1.5B probes with mean alignment 0.84 0.84 ; we report references that partially overlap with the training samples separately. Training samples at temperature 1.0 1.0 with top- p p 0.95 0.95 , whereas validation uses greedy decoding. Evaluating accuracy on the same fixed prompts ties the local objective more directly to measured performance, which is what the matched-compute studies do ( Section 2.3 ). Statistical resolution. The primary comparison, with n = 14 n=14 , establishes equivalence within ± 0.03 \pm 0.03 greedy accuracy, so smaller benefits and harms remain compatible with the data. These seed intervals are conditional on the chosen validation set. A larger fixed test set, separate sampled and greedy evaluations, and replication with independent seeds would each address a different source of uncertainty. Breadth and horizon. The amount of replication varies across the five-family probe panel, and most secondary training comparisons use n ≤ 5 n\leq 5 . The SmolLM3 endpoints and the positive single-seed Phi-4 pilots are descriptive evidence only, and the expansion of the gate study to n = 32 n=32 includes eight exploratory seeds. The experiments use six segments, a constant dense weight, and short training horizons. Other segmentations, schedules, datasets, initial policies, and longer horizons remain open. Reward integration. Our implicit-reward control uses the log ratio between the actor and the reference policy, whereas PRIME uses a separately trained reward model in the numerator. The learned-PRM study uses one particular segmentation and a reconstructed scoring head. Finally, masking tokens and then centering credit within each completion redistributes credit to the tokens that were not masked. Gates should therefore be evaluated after every transformation the trainer applies, and a controlled allocation experiment must also match the chosen credit or update budget. Appendix C Matched-Compute Studies This appendix documents the matched-compute studies of Sections 3 and 4 . Section C.1 describes the setup that all studies share, and Section C.2 lists every contrast and every study. Sections C.3 , C.4 , C.5 , C.6 and C.7 then follow the questions of the main text. Each opens with what the studies show together and continues with one record per study: its design, its preregistered contrasts, its checks, and the secondary results that the tables do not show. C.1 Setup Why a separate implementation. The equal-iteration comparisons of Section 3.1 match seeds and training iterations, so the Monte-Carlo reward uses more compute there than outcome-only GRPO: every scored prefix needs continuation rollouts. To compare the methods at the compute they consume, we reimplemented the training pipeline independently of the equal-iteration code, counted every generated token toward a matched budget B B (in this appendix, B B denotes this budget, not the matrix of Appendix I ), and extended the comparison to a learned critic and to prompt selection on six models. Together the studies comprise 1,668 1{,}668 runs and 1,468 1{,}468 GPU-hours, with one GPU per run. All ran on H200 GPUs except S35 ( 105 105 GPU-hours) and 22 22 GPU-hours of task studies, which ran on A100 GPUs. Implementation. Rollouts are sampled with vLLM in bf16; the trainer keeps float32 master weights and Adam states with bf16 autocast compute. With pure bf16 weights, Adam’s per-element step at these learning rates is smaller than half a bf16 unit in the last place for typical weights, so most weights would not change: in a check on Qwen2.5-0.5B, 87 % 87\% of parameters were unchanged after one step. The equal-iteration study’s recipe at learning rate 10 − 5 10^{-5} degraded or collapsed every configuration, outcome-only included, over the matched horizon. A preregistered stability probe, on a development set disjoint from the training pools and test sets, selected 10 − 6 10^{-6} ; a rate of 3 × 10 − 6 3\times 10^{-6} failed the probe. For Qwen3, a preregistered truncation rule raised the generation limit from 380 380 to 768 768 tokens. Qwen3-4B and Llama-3.2 runs use a microbatch size of 1 1 to fit in memory, which changes neither the update nor the token count. Llama-3.2 runs also fix the date that the chat template inserts. Each iteration trains on 8 8 prompts with 8 8 completions each, sampled at temperature 1 1 and top- p p 1 1 , and takes one optimizer step on them, with no ratio clipping or reuse of rollouts. The group advantage is the reward minus the group mean, divided by the group’s population standard deviation; the loss sums the token terms and divides by the number of completions in the iteration. The optimizer is Adam ( β = ( 0.9 , 0.999 ) \beta=(0.9,0.999) , ϵ = 10 − 8 \epsilon=10^{-8} ) without weight decay, with gradient clipping at norm 1 1 , no KL penalty, and a constant learning rate without warmup, so a run’s result at B B does not depend on whether it continues past B B (only S32 decays the rate, linearly to zero at B B ). The token budget B B counts all tokens generated during training: rollouts, continuations, and the discarded groups of dynamic sampling. A preregistered calibration rule set it for each model and task before the studies: 48 48 times the mean number of tokens per iteration that the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward generates over iterations 0–2 of one calibration run, rounded up to a multiple of 250,000 250{,}000 . The densest reward thus gets 50 50 to 57 57 updates, and outcome-only GRPO gets 584 584 on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 755 755 on Qwen3-4B. Models and budgets. The models are Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct ( 380 380 -token completions, B = 12.25 B=12.25 M); Qwen3 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B in non-thinking mode ( 768 768 tokens, B = 17.25 B=17.25 M, 21.0 21.0 M, and 22.25 22.25 M); and, for prompt selection only, Llama-3.2-1B- and 3B-Instruct ( 768 768 tokens, B = 10.0 B=10.0 M and 8.25 8.25 M). Each iteration trains on 8 8 prompts with 8 8 completions each. Pools and evaluation. All problems have plain numeric answers, so the last-number rule is well defined. Training uses MATH train problems of levels 4–5 ( 562 562 prompts after removing overlaps with the evaluation sets) or levels 3–5 ( 3,486 3{,}486 prompts, 3,458 3{,}458 used). Evaluation uses 200 200 MATH test problems of levels 3–5, every B / 16 B/16 tokens. The level-5 (S14, S18, S19) and level 1–2 (S21) studies train and evaluate on those levels only, with their own 200 200 -problem test sets. S33 and S34 also score two further test sets, only at the initial evaluation and at the first evaluation at or after B B . These scores are computed after the primary evaluation and with their own common random numbers, so they cannot affect training. The first holds the 1,543 1{,}543 MATH test problems of levels 3–5 with numeric answers of at most 1,000 1{,}000 characters that are in no other evaluation set. Of 2,295 2{,}295 such problems of any length, 623 623 are in earlier evaluation sets, 12 12 are longer, and 117 117 are near-duplicates of the level 3–5 training pool or the 200 200 (exact match after normalization, or 8 8 -gram Jaccard similarity of at least 0.6 0.6 , the check used to clean the training pools). The second is the GSM8K test set ( 1,319 1{,}319 problems), on which neither configuration trains. At each evaluation the policy draws 8 8 completions per problem at temperature 1 1 and top- p p 1 1 , with common random numbers across configurations of the same seed. The primary metric is accuracy at the first evaluation at or after B B , with no checkpoint selection. Greedy accuracy and strict-verifier accuracy, which counts only boxed answers, are secondary metrics. Statistics. The unit is a seed, or a (model size, seed) pair when sizes are pooled. We use paired t t -tests, Holm adjustment within each preregistered primary family, and two one-sided tests for equivalence within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 ( ± 0.01 \pm 0.01 in S28 and S31–S33, where the gain under test is 0.008 0.008 ). A crossed seed-by-problem bootstrap, which resamples seeds and test problems together, serves as a robustness check. Secondary contrasts are not corrected for multiple comparisons. Non-inferiority means that the lower bound of the 90 % 90\% interval exceeds − 0.02 -0.02 . Studies S15, S25, S26, and S32–S35 ran a smoke stage before their main runs, which checked that each configuration differed from its comparator only in the intended setting and that paired configurations shared their first iteration. Each preregistration also fixed how each possible outcome would be read, and the last sentence of each study below gives that reading. Because some of the gains we find are smaller than these margins, we report intervals throughout, alongside the equivalence tests. Preregistration. Before its runs, each study preregistered its hypotheses, primary contrasts, Holm families, equivalence margin, budgets, and seeds. The preregistration files are kept in the project repository, so their timing rests on file-system records. For 27 27 of the 34 34 files whose studies have main training runs, the last modification precedes the study’s first main training run. Of the other seven, six changed only in comments, and S2’s two dated amendments added rules for parts of the study before those parts ran. Calibration, pilot, and smoke stages may start before a file is final (S24, S27, and S35), and dated amendments, such as S1’s change of learning rate after its pilot, are recorded in the files. Monte-Carlo reward (S1, S25). The completion is cut into six segments at fixed token offsets. Continuations start at the five interior cuts, with k = 4 k=4 draws each ( k = 1 k=1 and 2 2 in two S25 configurations), and each continuation may use the rest of the completion’s token limit. The segment credit p ^ s − p ^ s − 1 \hat{p}_{s}-\hat{p}_{s-1} is added, without centering, to the group advantage. Because the cuts are at fixed offsets and the continuations run to the token limit, the credit targets the same reward as outcome-only GRPO, accuracy at the end of the completion: its expected contribution for a prompt is the gradient of that prompt’s success probability. Normalized GRPO’s expected update weights that gradient by λ G ​ ( p ) \lambda_{G}(p) , which varies across prompts ( Proposition 4 ), so adding the credit can change how the gradients of different prompts are weighted relative to each other. The equal-iteration study’s rule, which centers the credit and normalizes it by its maximum, serves as a diagnostic configuration with the same continuations. Learned critic (S2–S5, S26). A ridge regression on the policy’s hidden states predicts the success probability of a prefix. It is refit every iteration on the rollouts of earlier iterations, so each iteration is scored by a critic fit on other iterations. The temporal-difference form credits a segment with the change in predicted value across it. The baseline form subtracts the predicted value at the start of the segment from the outcome. Because the prediction depends only on the prefix and is fit on earlier iterations, this baseline does not change the expectation of the score-function estimator for the final reward. Both forms are added to the group advantage, which, as for the Monte-Carlo reward, can change how the gradients of different prompts are weighted relative to each other. At the initial Qwen2.5-1.5B policy, over 29,875 29{,}875 prefix cuts, the cross-fitted critic’s squared error against the prefix value is 0.057 0.057 , where the value’s variance across cuts is 0.076 0.076 and the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo estimate’s error is 0.010 0.010 ; its Brier score against outcomes is 0.101 0.101 , against 0.122 0.122 for a constant predictor. The critic is thus far noisier than the Monte-Carlo reward, but it needs no continuations. Prompt filters (S6–S24, S27, S28, S30, S31). Each iteration draws 24 24 candidates from the seed’s prompt stream, 3 3 times the 8 8 prompts it trains on. The stream is the pool shuffled once per epoch and consumed in order, so a filter passes through it three times as fast as outcome-only GRPO. A candidate’s predicted success p p comes from one of three sources. The critic filter uses a ridge critic on the hidden state at the end of the prompt, refit on the group mean rewards of the previous 32 32 iterations. The history filter uses the prompt’s own mean group reward from the last iteration in which it was trained, or the recent mean reward for prompts not yet trained. The level filter uses the recent mean reward of the prompt’s MATH level. The trainer keeps the 6 6 candidates with p p nearest the target, 1 / 2 1/2 by default, plus 2 2 random others. Until 32 32 prompts have been trained, it takes the first 8 8 candidates. The random-choice control (S11–S13, S15, S20) fits and logs the critic filter but trains on 8 8 of the 24 24 candidates drawn uniformly at random. Because the stream depends only on the seed and the iteration, the control and the filter of a seed score the same candidates in every iteration. DAPO-style dynamic sampling generates groups in stream order and keeps those with mixed outcomes until it has 8 8 ; every discarded group still counts toward B B . Length reward (S6–S9). Some early configurations added a length term relative to the group. Every token of a correct completion has its credit changed by − λ ( L i − m g ) / L max -\lambda(L_{i}-m_{g})/L_{\max} , where L i L_{i} is the completion’s length, m g m_{g} the mean length of the correct completions in its group, and L max L_{\max} the token limit. Wrong completions are unchanged, and the term sums to zero over each group. We report this term only because of the drift it caused. Verifiers. The default verifier follows the equal-iteration study’s rule. It reads the last number in the final boxed expression, or, if there is no boxed expression, the last number in the completion. The strict verifier requires a boxed answer. An evaluation counts as drifted when default-verifier accuracy exceeds strict-verifier accuracy by more than 0.1 0.1 . C.3 Dense rewards at matched compute These studies ask whether the per-update advantage of dense rewards survives once their continuations are paid for, and, if not, whether a cheaper estimate closes the gap. The Monte-Carlo reward spends 91 % 91\% of its generated tokens on continuations, so by the time it reaches B B it has made 50 50 updates to outcome-only GRPO’s 584 584 . That cost decides the comparison on every model we trained ( Table 6 ). Fewer continuations per cut shrink the deficit, in the order the per-token gradient test predicts, because continuation sampling adds little noise to the update (S25). The heuristic extrapolation from that test overestimates all five rewards we checked by 0.008 0.008 to 0.018 0.018 on average over the evaluations, and every interval excludes zero. This error is small next to the Monte-Carlo rewards’ extrapolated deficits, so their order survives, but it is as large as the critics’ extrapolated leads. A learned critic, which needs no continuations, matches outcome-only GRPO within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 (S26); its temporal-difference form shortens completions, by 16 % 16\% [ 6 , 24 ] [6,24] on Qwen3 and 3 % 3\% [ 1 , 6 ] [1,6] on Qwen2.5-1.5B. The equal-iteration study’s rule for combining credit, which centers it and normalizes it by its maximum, is equivalent to ours with the same continuations ( Table 6 ), so the result does not hinge on that choice. No Monte-Carlo reward we can afford, with k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 and six segments, matches outcome-only GRPO at this budget, and the designs that reuse samples or train past the budget leave the ranking unchanged ( Appendices E and F ). C.3.1 First studies (S1–S10) Table 6 gives the source of every number from S1–S10 that Sections 3 and 4 quote and that later sections of this appendix do not report. Gradient efficiencies were measured on 96 96 fixed batches of 8 × 8 8\times 8 rollouts at the initial Qwen2.5-1.5B policy, relative to outcome-only GRPO.
