(S25) S25 repeats the S1 protocol (Qwen2.5-1.5B, level 4–5 pool, B = 12.25 B=12.25 M) on fresh seeds 5–16 with four configurations: outcome-only GRPO and the Monte-Carlo reward with k = 4 k=4 , 2 2 , and 1 1 continuations per interior cut. The prediction. A second gradient measurement scored rewards with k = 1 k=1 to 16 16 on 96 96 batches at the initial policy, against the same reference gradient as the first measurement but with separately sampled rollouts; its values for k = 4 k=4 ( 1.63 × 1.63\times per update and 0.15 × 0.15\times per token) differ from the first measurement’s ( 1.56 × 1.56\times and 0.145 × 0.145\times ) because of this resampling. Per update, the rewards with k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , 4 4 , 8 8 , and 16 16 are 1.55 × 1.55\times , 1.60 × 1.60\times , 1.63 × 1.63\times , 1.67 × 1.67\times , and 1.69 × 1.69\times as efficient as outcome-only GRPO; per generated token, they are 0.45 × 0.45\times [ 0.35 , 0.63 ] [0.35,0.63] , 0.27 × 0.27\times [ 0.21 , 0.38 ] [0.21,0.38] , 0.15 × 0.15\times [ 0.12 , 0.21 ] [0.12,0.21] , 0.08 × 0.08\times , and 0.04 × 0.04\times . Fitting the update’s second moment as a + b / k a+b/k attributes 3.8 % 3.8\% [ 3.4 , 4.2 ] [3.4,4.2] of it at k = 4 k=4 to continuation sampling. Therefore, no allocation of the k = 4 k=4 continuations across cuts can raise the per-update efficiency by more than a factor of 1.04 1.04 . The preregistration predicted that every Monte-Carlo reward trails outcome-only GRPO and that the rewards order as k = 1 > 2 > 4 k=1>2>4 . Descriptive. At B B , the configurations have made 599 599 , 163 163 , 92 92 , and 50 50 updates (outcome-only, k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , 4 4 ). Continuations take 70 % 70\% , 83 % 83\% , and 91 % 91\% of the Monte-Carlo rewards’ tokens. The share of cut-off samples falls from 74 % 74\% at the start to 22 % 22\% , 46 % 46\% , 58 % 58\% , and 67 % 67\% , so the rewards with fewer updates keep more of the initial policy’s long completions. Its lead over k = 4 k=4 grows to 0.081 0.081 at B B . A heuristic extrapolation (exploratory). If a reward’s per-token efficiency e e held throughout training, training with it would reach, after t t tokens, the accuracy that outcome-only GRPO reaches after e ​ t et tokens. This is a heuristic, not a consequence of the analysis. Read off outcome-only GRPO’s curve on the same seed, it gives 0.359 0.359 , 0.319 0.319 , and 0.275 0.275 at B B for k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , and 4 4 . The rewards reached 0.343 0.343 , 0.299 0.299 , and 0.263 0.263 , against outcome-only GRPO’s 0.412 0.412 . Averaged over all 16 16 evaluations, observed minus extrapolated accuracy is − 0.017 -0.017 [ − 0.023 , − 0.012 ] [-0.023,-0.012] , − 0.013 -0.013 [ − 0.020 , − 0.006 ] [-0.020,-0.006] , and − 0.008 -0.008 [ − 0.013 , − 0.002 ] [-0.013,-0.002] . Applied to the critics of Section 3.3 (seeds 0–4), the same extrapolation uses only the evaluations at which e ​ t et stays within the length of the outcome-only run: up to 11 ​ B / 16 11B/16 for the temporal-difference form and up to 13 ​ B / 16 13B/16 for the baseline form. It gives leads of + 0.020 +0.020 and + 0.008 +0.008 ; the critics led by + 0.003 +0.003 [ − 0.012 , + 0.018 ] [-0.012,+0.018] and + 0.000 +0.000 . Observed minus extrapolated is − 0.017 -0.017 [ − 0.031 , − 0.003 ] [-0.031,-0.003] and − 0.008 -0.008 [ − 0.020 , + 0.005 ] [-0.020,+0.005] . The extrapolation thus overestimates all five rewards by similar amounts. S26 then tested the critics’ shortfall as a preregistered prediction on 24 24 fresh seeds (next subsection), and it held. The efficiency is measured at the initial policy and describes the first-order gain of an SGD step tuned to each reward, whereas training uses Adam at one fixed learning rate. C.3.3 Learned critic on fresh seeds (S26) S26 repeated the S4 critic comparison on Qwen2.5-1.5B (level 4–5 pool, 380 380 tokens, B = 12.25 B=12.25 M, the same configuration files) on seeds 5–28, after S25 suggested that the extrapolation overestimates the critics. Outcome-only seeds 5–16 are the S25 runs, finished before S26 was designed; seeds 17–28 were trained in S26. The primary family was equivalence within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 for both forms (TOST, Holm over the two). The preregistration also specified each primary contrast’s two-sided paired t t , Holm-adjusted over the two, and that a form significantly above zero would count as a gain at matched tokens. The temporal-difference form’s greedy completions are 3 % 3\% [ 1 , 6 ] [1,6] shorter, and 18.7 % 18.7\% of its samples are truncated against 22.4 % 22.4\% . Both critics are in use from iteration 4 on. C.4 Does prompt selection help? Prompt selection spends compute differently from dense rewards. One forward pass over 24 24 candidate prompts per iteration decides which 8 8 prompts to sample, and scoring generates no tokens. Curriculum methods that train on prompts with predicted success near 1 / 2 1/2 use the same rule ( Gao et al., 2026 ; Qu et al., 2026 ; Sun et al., 2025 ) , so we test the rule rather than propose it. It gains on two of six models with the level 3–5 pool, Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen2.5-1.5B, and both gains replicate on fresh seeds ( Table 3 ). The studies below probe the gain from four sides. Fresh seeds and further test sets ask whether it is real (S12, S20, S33, S34). Random choice among the same candidates asks whether it comes from the choice of prompts or from moving faster through the prompt stream (S11–S13, S15, S20). Simpler estimates of a prompt’s success ask whether the learned critic is needed (S7–S9, S22). Other models and pools ask how far the gain extends (S13, S14, and Section C.5 ). The answers are consistent. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, the gain carries over to 1,543 1{,}543 MATH test problems that no study had evaluated on, with an interval that also resamples the problems, and to GSM8K, which neither configuration trains on; the same holds on Qwen3-0.6B. On both models, the choice of prompts carries the gain: random choice among the same candidates is equivalent to outcome-only GRPO (on Qwen3-0.6B, + 0.008 +0.008 [ − 0.003 , + 0.019 ] [-0.003,+0.019] over 18 18 seeds from two studies, TOST p = .014 p=.014 ), and it trails the filter in every preregistered primary contrast. A per-prompt success estimate near 1 / 2 1/2 carries most of the gain at B B even without a learned critic, and the critic adds an early lead. A filter built from the MATH difficulty level alone gains nothing, and dynamic sampling loses at matched tokens, because the groups it discards still count toward the budget. The gain on this model also carries over to the further problems. C.4.8 A filter without a critic on Qwen2.5-1.5B (S22) S22 asked whether the Qwen2.5-1.5B gain needs a learned critic. Its one new configuration is the S15/S20 filter configuration with the history estimate in place of the critic: the same candidates, rule, warm-up, and random picks. It ran on the same 36 36 seeds, paired with the S15 and S20 runs of each seed. The history filter has no per-prompt estimate until a prompt has been trained. A filter run scores about 15,200 15{,}200 candidates before B B , 4.4 4.4 passes through the 3,458 3{,}458 prompts of the stream, so its first pass ends near 0.23 ​ B 0.23B . Holm adjustment covered two primary contrasts: • History filter minus outcome, with no directional prediction: + 0.0060 +0.0060 [ 0.0011 , 0.0110 ] [0.0011,0.0110] (Holm p = .035 p=.035 ; higher in 22 22 of 36 36 ; crossed bootstrap [ − 0.002 , + 0.014 ] [-0.002,+0.014] ). • Critic filter minus history filter, predicted positive: + 0.0024 +0.0024 [ − 0.0020 , + 0.0068 ] [-0.0020,+0.0068] (Holm p = .27 p=.27 ; higher in 21 21 of 36 36 ; crossed [ − 0.006 , + 0.010 ] [-0.006,+0.010] ), equivalent to zero within ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 (TOST p < 10 − 9 p<10^{-9} ). Secondary results: • Over the first half of the budget, the critic filter led the history filter by 0.019 0.019 [ 0.016 , 0.021 ] [0.016,0.021] on average, in all 36 36 seeds. By evaluation, its lead was + 0.020 +0.020 at B / 8 B/8 , peaked at + 0.026 +0.026 at B / 4 B/4 , and fell to + 0.002 +0.002 at B B . Over the same half, the history filter led outcome-only GRPO by + 0.002 +0.002 [ − 0.001 , + 0.004 ] [-0.001,+0.004] . • The history filter led random choice from its candidates by + 0.007 +0.007 [ 0.002 , 0.012 ] [0.002,0.012] ( p = .010 p=.010 ). • Strict-verifier accuracy: + 0.017 +0.017 [ − 0.001 , + 0.035 ] [-0.001,+0.035] over outcome-only, carried by the two drifted outcome-only runs ( + 0.236 +0.236 and + 0.204 +0.204 on seeds 30 and 32). • The history filter raised mixed groups by 4.8 4.8 points [ 4.4 , 5.2 ] [4.4,5.2] , higher in every seed, about half the critic filter’s 9.8 9.8 on the same seeds. Descriptive: its candidates’ estimated success averaged 0.33 0.33 , and its chosen prompts’ 0.37 0.37 . Its mean training reward before B B was 0.40 0.40 , against 0.37 0.37 for outcome-only GRPO and 0.46 0.46 for the critic filter. It trained on 3,444 3{,}444 distinct prompts per run on average, against the critic filter’s 2,605 2{,}605 . A per-prompt success estimate near 1 / 2 1/2 thus carries the gain at B B without a learned critic. The interval on the critic filter’s advantage at B B , [ − 0.002 , + 0.007 ] [-0.002,+0.007] , does not exclude a difference nearly as large as the critic filter’s own gain ( + 0.008 +0.008 ). On this model, the critic’s clearest contribution is the early lead. C.5 Where does prompt selection help? The filter changes the trained groups in every setting: it raises the share of mixed-outcome groups, the only groups with a nonzero GRPO advantage, by 8 8 to 19 19 points. Accuracy, however, rises in only some settings, so the composition of the groups does not predict the gain. On Qwen3-0.6B, the filter aimed at predicted success 0.85 0.85 trains on about the same share of mixed groups as outcome-only GRPO ( + 0.5 +0.5 points, compared with + 0.3 +0.3 for random choice) and still gains as much as the filter aimed at 1 / 2 1/2 ( Table 4 ). The share of all-wrong groups does not predict the gain either: on Qwen3-0.6B the target 0.85 0.85 cuts it from 36 % 36\% to 15 % 15\% and gains, while on Qwen3-1.7B it cuts it from 25 % 25\% to 10 % 10\% and gains nothing ( + 0.001 +0.001 , n = 6 n=6 ). What separates the settings is how hard the candidates are. In the five settings from which we formed this account, post hoc, the critic’s mean predicted success over the 24 24 candidates was 0.42 0.42 and 0.40 0.40 where the filter helped and 0.54 0.54 to 0.70 0.70 where it did not, with no overlap between seeds ( Table 8 ). When the candidates’ mean prediction is below 1 / 2 1/2 , keeping those nearest 1 / 2 1/2 moves training toward easier prompts and away from all-wrong groups; when it is above 1 / 2 1/2 , the filter moves training toward harder prompts. The training reward shows the move. Wherever the filter helped, its mean training reward exceeded outcome-only GRPO’s ( 0.43 0.43 – 0.47 0.47 against 0.30 0.30 – 0.39 0.39 ), and in the six settings without a gain whose candidates averaged above 0.53 0.53 , it fell below. Outcome-only GRPO’s own training reward, which needs no critic, separates the first eleven settings in the same way, whereas the base model’s accuracy on its first training prompts, known before training, does not. Six preregistered studies then tested the account ( Table 1 ). Two moved the candidates across 1 / 2 1/2 by changing the pool (S19, S21), two changed the model family (S24, S27), and two kept model and pool fixed and reversed only the direction of the move, by aiming the filter at 0.25 0.25 (S30, S31). Five of the six predictions held. The gain predicted on Llama-3.2-1B did not appear at B B , although the filter led at every evaluation after the first. Across all twelve settings, the filter gained significantly in four of the five settings whose candidates averaged below 1 / 2 1/2 and in none of the seven above. Candidates below 1 / 2 1/2 thus mark where a gain is possible, and on a fixed model and pool the direction of the move decides whether it appears. C.5.1 Candidate difficulty and a test on Qwen3-1.7B with level 5 (S19) Averaged over the iterations before B B in which the critic was in use, the critic’s mean predicted success over the 24 24 candidates separated, in the five settings from which the account was formed, the settings in which the filter helped from those in which it did not (S27 later found a setting below 1 / 2 1/2 without a significant gain). It is 0.419 0.419 (Qwen3-0.6B, 54 54 seeds, range 0.404 0.404 – 0.430 0.430 ) and 0.397 0.397 (Qwen2.5-1.5B, 60 60 seeds, 0.383 0.383 – 0.409 0.409 ) where the filter helped, against 0.587 0.587 (Qwen3-1.7B, 0.578 0.578 – 0.596 0.596 ), 0.544 0.544 (Qwen3-4B on level 5, 0.533 0.533 – 0.558 0.558 ), and 0.701 0.701 (Qwen3-4B on levels 3–5, 0.688 0.688 – 0.717 0.717 ) where it did not. Below 1 / 2 1/2 , the filter moves training toward easier prompts and away from all-wrong groups ( 36 % → 24 % 36\%\to 24\% on Qwen3-0.6B, 39 % → 27 % 39\%\to 27\% on Qwen2.5-1.5B). Above 1 / 2 1/2 , it moves training toward harder prompts and mainly removes all-correct groups. The level-5 study on 4B did not test this account, because 4B’s candidates there were still above 1 / 2 1/2 on average. S19, preregistered after this observation, trains Qwen3-1.7B, the model without a gain on levels 3–5, on the level-5 pool and test set ( 24 24 seeds). Its precondition is a candidate mean below 1 / 2 1/2 , and its primary prediction is a gain. The precondition held: the candidates’ mean predicted success was 0.389 0.389 (seed range 0.377 0.377 – 0.407 0.407 ), and the chosen prompts’ 0.457 0.457 . The single primary contrast, filter minus outcome-only at B B , was + 0.0112 +0.0112 [ 0.0020 , 0.0205 ] [0.0020,0.0205] ( p = .019 p=.019 ; higher in 17 17 of 24 24 ; crossed bootstrap [ − 0.0003 , + 0.024 ] [-0.0003,+0.024] ). Its 90 % 90\% interval, [ 0.004 , 0.019 ] [0.004,0.019] , also lies inside ± 0.02 \pm 0.02 (TOST p = .031 p=.031 ), so the gain is significant but smaller than two points. Strict-verifier accuracy moved the same way ( + 0.010 +0.010 [ 0.001 , 0.019 ] [0.001,0.019] , p = .025 p=.025 , secondary). As on Qwen3-0.6B, the filter removed mainly all-wrong groups ( 45 % → 30 % 45\%\to 30\% ; all-correct 17 % → 19 % 17\%\to 19\% ) and raised mixed groups by 12.7 12.7 points [ 12.1 , 13.3 ] [12.1,13.3] , higher in every seed. The lead rose to + 0.018 +0.018 at 5 ​ B / 16 5B/16 and stayed between + 0.007 +0.007 and + 0.014 +0.014 from B / 2 B/2 to B B . Training started from accuracy 0.294 0.294 and reached 0.409 0.409 (outcome-only) and 0.420 0.420 (filter) at B B . The account thus predicted a gain in a model that had none on levels 3–5, a confirmatory test of an account formed post hoc on five settings. The filter at 0.25 0.25 drifted in 5 5 of 408 408 evaluations, against 16 16 (outcome-only GRPO) and 1 1 (filter at 1 / 2 1/2 ) of 408 408 on the same seeds; one of its runs (seed 21) and two outcome-only runs (seeds 30 and 32) were drifted at B B . Without the filter’s drifted run, T1 is − 0.0086 -0.0086 [ − 0.0146 , − 0.0025 ] [-0.0146,-0.0025] and T2 + 0.0007 +0.0007 [ − 0.0064 , + 0.0079 ] [-0.0064,+0.0079] ; without all three, − 0.0073 -0.0073 [ − 0.0137 , − 0.0010 ] [-0.0137,-0.0010] and + 0.0024 +0.0024 [ − 0.0050 , + 0.0099 ] [-0.0050,+0.0099] (exploratory). Training took 0.49 0.49 hours per run on H200 GPUs. The direction of the move thus mattered on a second model; the early lead shows that training on prompts harder than the candidates, while still avoiding those predicted near 0 0 , kept a small part of the filter’s head start on this model, but none of its gain at B B . C.5.7 Candidates, calibration, and training reward (exploratory) Table 8 lists every setting with a critic-filter configuration and an outcome-only configuration on the same seeds, ordered by the candidates’ mean predicted success. The first studies on the level 4–5 pool (S6, S7; 6 6 – 8 8 seeds) were not among the five settings on which the account of Section 4.2 was formed, and S19, S21, S24, and S27 tested it; we add all of them here. Training rewards are means over the iterations started before B B , under the verifier that trained the configuration (strict for the Llama models, default otherwise). In all five settings below 1 / 2 1/2 the filter raised the training reward; it gained significantly in four, and on Llama-3.2-1B its + 0.004 +0.004 [ − 0.001 , + 0.010 ] [-0.001,+0.010] was not significant. In the six settings above 0.53 0.53 it lowered the training reward (by 0.01 0.01 on Qwen3-4B on level 5, by 0.05 0.05 to 0.15 0.15 in the others) and did not gain; Qwen3-1.7B on levels 4–5 sits at 0.52 0.52 , with the filter’s training reward 0.01 0.01 above outcome-only GRPO’s and an inconclusive estimate. Outcome-only GRPO’s training reward, which needs no critic, orders the Qwen settings as the candidates’ predicted success does. In the first eleven settings it separated the seeds of the settings with and without a gain without overlap; on Llama-3.2-1B it fell in the range of the settings with a gain, so it, too, predicted the gain that S27 did not find. The rewards of the first iteration, before any update, do not separate the settings: Qwen3-4B on level 5 ( 0.30 0.30 ) falls inside the range of the settings with a gain ( 0.18 0.18 – 0.35 0.35 ), Llama-3.2-3B ( 0.39 0.39 ) just above it, and Llama-3.2-1B ( 0.13 0.13 ) below it. 0.011 , + 0.000 ] -0.005\,[-0.011,+0.000] Table 9 checks the critic’s predictions against the groups it trained on. Random choice trains on a uniform draw of the candidates, so its realized minus predicted success measures calibration on the candidate distribution: it is within 0.2 0.2 points on all three models. On level 1–2 problems and on Llama-3.2-3B, which have no random-choice configuration, the filter’s trained prompts succeeded 1.2 1.2 and 0.9 0.9 points more often than predicted; there the critic, if anything, underestimated how far above 1 / 2 1/2 they lay. On Llama-3.2-1B they succeeded as often as predicted ( + 0.08 +0.08 points [ − 0.05 , + 0.21 ] [-0.05,+0.21] ), so its candidates’ side of 1 / 2 1/2 is not an artifact of calibration. A prompt’s prediction correlates with its group’s success at 0.67 0.67 to 0.72 0.72 , although a group has only eight rollouts. On the filter’s chosen prompts, which lie near 1 / 2 1/2 , the correlation is lower because their range is narrower. The threshold of 1 / 2 1/2 in the account is therefore a threshold on realized success, not only on the critic’s scale. Where we followed the gain over time, it was a head start ( Figure 5 ). On Qwen3-0.6B, it peaked at 5 ​ B / 8 5B/8 and had mostly faded by 2 ​ B 2B (S16, S17). On Qwen2.5-1.5B ( 60 60 seeds), it peaked at + 0.026 +0.026 [ 0.022 , 0.030 ] [0.022,0.030] at 3 ​ B / 16 3B/16 and fell to + 0.008 +0.008 at B B . On Qwen3-4B with level 5, it peaked at + 0.042 +0.042 [ 0.026 , 0.057 ] [0.026,0.057] at B / 8 B/8 (S14). A head start is a saving in compute: where the filter gains, it reaches outcome-only GRPO’s accuracy at B B with 22 22 to 23 % 23\% fewer generated tokens and 13 13 to 17 % 17\% less training time ( Table 11 ). The Qwen3-0.6B gain also holds at pass@8 ( + 0.009 +0.009 [ 0.002 , 0.017 ] [0.002,0.017] , n = 54 n=54 ), which cuts the share of test problems that no sample solves from 27.1 % 27.1\% to 26.2 % 26.2\% , so the filter does more than sharpen sampling on problems the policy already solves ( Table 10 ). Because the gain is a head start, a faster baseline is its natural rival, and a doubled or decaying learning rate tests it directly. On Qwen3-0.6B, doubling outcome-only GRPO’s learning rate buys as much as the filter, with and without drift (S23, S29). On Qwen2.5-1.5B, a doubled constant rate lowers both configurations and the filter’s gain holds (S28), while a rate decaying from 2 × 10 − 6 2\times 10^{-6} to 0 0 at B B brings outcome-only GRPO within half a point of the filter and removes the filter’s own gain at B B (S32). The filter’s gain is thus about as large as what tuning the baseline’s learning rate buys, which is why a claimed gain should be measured against a tuned baseline. C.6.1 Twice the budget (S16) On Qwen3-0.6B, the filter’s lead grew over the budget ( + 0.011 +0.011 at B / 4 B/4 , + 0.020 +0.020 at B / 2 B/2 , and + 0.024 +0.024 at B B over seeds 0–17), and outcome-only accuracy was still rising at B B . C.6.6 A decaying learning rate on Qwen2.5-1.5B (S32) S28’s faster runs rose quickly and then stalled. S32 gives both S28 configurations a learning rate that decays linearly with generated tokens. C.6.7 Pass@ k k and test levels (exploratory) Each evaluation stores, per test problem, the number c c of correct samples out of n = 8 n=8 . Table 10 gives the unbiased estimate 1 − ( n − c k ) / ( n k ) 1-\binom{n-c}{k}/\binom{n}{k} of pass@ k k , averaged over problems, as paired differences at B B . Pass@8 equals one minus the share of problems that no sample solves. On Qwen3-0.6B ( 54 54 seeds), the filter’s gain over outcome-only holds at every level of the test set. It is + 0.015 +0.015 [ 0.008 , 0.022 ] [0.008,0.022] on the 79 79 level-3 problems, + 0.024 +0.024 [ 0.016 , 0.032 ] [0.016,0.032] on the 62 62 level-4 problems, and + 0.011 +0.011 [ 0.006 , 0.017 ] [0.006,0.017] on the 59 59 level-5 problems. With both configurations on the strict reward, the gain at B B again holds at pass@8, and by 2 ​ B 2B it is gone at every k k . On Qwen2.5-1.5B, the gain shrinks as k k grows, and pass@8 does not differ. On Qwen3-0.6B on levels 1–2, where the filter trains on harder prompts, its small pass@1 deficit becomes a small pass@8 surplus; neither is significant. On Llama-3.2-3B, where it also trains on harder prompts, the deficit instead grows with k k , and at pass@8 its interval just excludes zero. On Llama-3.2-1B, as on Qwen2.5-1.5B, the small pass@1 lead shrinks at larger k k . C.7 Reward verification and drift When no answer is boxed, the default verifier falls back to the last number in a completion, so it also credits unboxed answers. Some runs trained on it drift away from boxing their answers; we count an evaluation as drifted when default-verifier accuracy exceeds strict-verifier accuracy by more than 0.1 0.1 . Table 12 counts drift by study and configuration. Drift appeared mainly in long or fast training: on Qwen3-4B with level 5, on Qwen3-0.6B over twice the budget or at twice the learning rate, and on Qwen2.5-1.5B in the replication and under both faster learning rates. It affected outcome-only GRPO more often than the filter, which may have inflated the filter’s lead at 2 ​ B 2B in S16. Elsewhere it was rare. The history filter of S22 drifted in 1 1 of 612 612 evaluations; on the Qwen2.5-1.5B level 4–5 pool, no Monte-Carlo, outcome-only, or baseline-critic run drifted (S25, S26), while the temporal-difference critic drifted at 5 5 of 408 408 evaluations, and 2 2 of its 24 24 runs ended drifted. On Qwen3-1.7B, drift appeared only under a length-reward term ( 5 5 of 3,717 3{,}717 evaluations over all finished runs), and under that term two of three Qwen3-4B runs stopped boxing their answers, with strict-verifier accuracy of 0.165 0.165 and 0.077 0.077 while default-verifier accuracy stayed high. One filter run and one outcome-only run of the S11 Qwen3-4B replication also ended drifted. Training on the strict verifier removes drift without lowering accuracy. In the studies that retrained drifting configurations on it (S17, S18), no run drifted ( 0 0 of 204 204 evaluations on Qwen3-4B and 0 0 of 1,584 1{,}584 on Qwen3-0.6B), and neither did outcome-only GRPO at twice the learning rate (S29, 0 0 of 408 408 ). On the two Llama models, which trained on the strict reward throughout, 1 1 of 1,632 1{,}632 evaluations drifted. Default-verifier accuracy was non-inferior in both preregistered tests, and strict-verifier accuracy rose, mostly on the seeds whose default-reward runs had drifted. We therefore report strict-verifier accuracy alongside default-verifier accuracy and recommend the strict verifier as the training reward. B , learning rate decaying from 2 × 10 − 6 2{\times}10^{-6} 16 / 408 16/408 ( 1 / 24 1/24 ) 5 / 408 5/408 ( 1 / 24 1/24 ) – C.8 Summary Matching compute reverses the ranking that the per-update gradient test gives: the densest reward ranks first per update and last per token, and a learned critic, which needs no continuations, matches outcome-only GRPO within two points. The same protocol confirms a real gain where one exists. Choosing prompts whose predicted success is nearest 1 / 2 1/2 improves accuracy on two models, replicates on fresh seeds and on test problems that no study had seen, comes from the choice of prompts, and follows a rule that predicted the outcome of five of six preregistered tests. The protocol also measures that gain precisely: it is a head start worth about a fifth of the budget, comparable to what a retuned learning rate buys. A claim that a dense reward or a prompt-selection rule helps should therefore state the budget it matches, the verifier that defines its reward, how its baseline was tuned, and the models on which it replicated. Appendix D Training-Time Accounting Generated tokens are not the only cost of an intervention. An inference engine generates continuations more cheaply per token than a training step processes rollouts, and a critic adds forward passes and fits that generate no tokens at all. We therefore also measure training time, stage by stage, and compare configurations at equal training time. Every run logs, for each iteration, the wall time of prompt selection (including the prompt critic’s prefill pass), generation of rollouts, credit (including Monte-Carlo continuations and the learned critic’s features and fits), the optimizer update, and the copy of the updated weights into the generation engine. Training time is the sum of these over the iterations before an evaluation; evaluation, logging, and checkpointing are timed separately and excluded. Each run uses one GPU (an H200, for every run in this section) on nodes shared with other jobs. We compare configurations only within a study or across studies run on the same GPU type, paired by seed. Equal-time contrasts (exploratory reanalyses of preregistered runs). For each seed, T ∗ T^{*} is the shorter of the two runs’ training times up to their evaluation at B B . Each run’s accuracy is interpolated linearly in training time at T ∗ T^{*} , so the configuration that reaches B B first is read at B B , and the other is read between two of its evaluations. These contrasts stop when the faster configuration reaches its budget. A Monte-Carlo reward’s runs end before outcome-only GRPO has trained as long as it needs to reach B B , so Appendix F gives every configuration that much time in a preregistered study. Table 13 lists every contrast, Table 14 gives the time per stage, and Figure 10 shows the training curves of the Monte-Carlo rewards and critics. The Monte-Carlo rewards spend most of their time on continuations (the credit stage), which the generation engine samples in large batches, whereas outcome-only GRPO spends most of its time on its many updates. A continuation token therefore costs less time than a rollout token that is also trained on, which is why the token budget charges the Monte-Carlo rewards more heavily than the time budget does. The learned critic’s features and fits add about a quarter to outcome-only GRPO’s training time, and the prompt critic’s prefill pass adds about a tenth. Appendix E Pilot Studies of Sample Reuse and Other Estimators (S3, S4) Two preregistered pilot studies tested ways to make dense rewards cheaper, as well as alternative estimators. They used Qwen2.5-1.5B with the protocol of S1 (MATH L4–5 pool, 380 380 tokens, B = 12.25 B=12.25 M, learning rate 10 − 6 10^{-6} ) and seeds 0–2 on H200 GPUs, and compared against the outcome-only and k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo runs of S2 on the same seeds. With three seeds, these are pilots: their intervals are wide, and only large effects are resolved. Continuation reuse at the gradient level. Training on the continuations themselves uses every generated token for training. Each continuation becomes a training sequence, with the prompt and the rollout prefix as context, and receives leave-one-out credit among the draws at its cut, in the style of SPO-tree and TreeRL. On 64 64 fixed batches, measured with the gradient efficiency of Section 3.3 , this was 6.4 × 6.4\times [ 3.3 , 18.8 ] [3.3,18.8] as efficient as outcome-only GRPO per update with k = 4 k=4 continuations per cut and 3.7 × 3.7\times [ 2.1 , 10.1 ] [2.1,10.1] with k = 2 k=2 , but 0.60 × 0.60\times [ 0.31 , 1.75 ] [0.31,1.75] and 0.64 × 0.64\times [ 0.35 , 1.73 ] [0.35,1.73] per generated token, against 0.22 × 0.22\times [ 0.15 , 0.43 ] [0.15,0.43] for the Monte-Carlo reward without reuse on the same batches. The preregistered condition for training it, a per-token efficiency of at least 0.8 0.8 , was not met. Rollout reuse and the VinePPO-style advantage. Accuracy at B B : outcome-only GRPO 0.408 0.408 , the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward 0.260 0.260 . Four clipped optimizer steps per batch (two epochs of two minibatches, clip 0.2 0.2 ) gave 0.337 0.337 for the reward ( + 0.077 +0.077 [ 0.049 , 0.105 ] [0.049,0.105] , Holm p = .007 p=.007 ) and 0.349 0.349 for outcome-only GRPO ( − 0.059 -0.059 [ − 0.102 , − 0.016 ] [-0.102,-0.016] , p = .027 p=.027 ); the preregistered interaction, the change in outcome-only GRPO’s lead, was − 0.136 -0.136 [ − 0.175 , − 0.097 ] [-0.175,-0.097] (Holm p = .009 p=.009 ). The VinePPO-style value advantage (Monte-Carlo value differences alone, with V ⁡ ( prompt ) V(\text{prompt}) the leave-one-out group mean) gave 0.258 0.258 , equivalent to the reward ( − 0.002 -0.002 [ − 0.024 , + 0.019 ] [-0.024,+0.019] , TOST p = .036 p=.036 ), and 0.337 0.337 with the same reuse ( + 0.000 +0.000 [ − 0.042 , + 0.043 ] [-0.042,+0.043] against the reused reward). REINFORCE with a leave-one-out baseline instead of the group-normalized advantage changed neither configuration materially (outcome-only 0.418 0.418 , Monte-Carlo reward 0.259 0.259 ). Process rewards from a frozen PRM. Process-supervised GRPO (adapted from DeepSeekMath) with the frozen Qwen2.5-Math-PRM-7B and without the verifier reward collapsed: the policy stopped boxing its answers ( 0.005 0.005 boxes per completion), and accuracy fell to 0.043 0.043 ( − 0.365 -0.365 [ − 0.389 , − 0.341 ] [-0.389,-0.341] ). Adding the outcome reward to the process rewards did not prevent the collapse ( 0.040 0.040 ). Used instead as a baseline for the outcome reward that depends only on the state, the same PRM was equivalent to outcome-only GRPO ( − 0.002 -0.002 [ − 0.023 , + 0.018 ] [-0.023,+0.018] , TOST p = .033 p=.033 ). Learning rate and group size. Outcome-only GRPO at 2 × 10 − 6 2\times 10^{-6} and 3 × 10 − 6 3\times 10^{-6} ended 0.034 0.034 [ 0.004 , 0.064 ] [0.004,0.064] and 0.111 0.111 [ − 0.003 , + 0.224 ] [-0.003,+0.224] below its run at 10 − 6 10^{-6} on this pool. Neither critic form gained over outcome-only GRPO at the same higher rate, with four optimizer steps per batch, or with groups of 2 2 or 4 4 completions (every contrast within [ − 0.019 , + 0.021 ] [-0.019,+0.021] , none significant). Appendix F Equal Training Time Past the Token Budget (S35) The equal-time contrasts of Appendix D stop at a Monte-Carlo reward’s own training time at B B , which is shorter than outcome-only GRPO’s. This study asks whether a reward catches up when given as much training time as outcome-only GRPO. A preregistered study (fixed before any of its training runs) answers this question with 768 768 -token completions, where 20 % 20\% of the initial samples are truncated instead of 73 % 73\% . It trains Qwen2.5-1.5B on the level 4–5 pool, with the strict verifier as every configuration’s training reward, learning rate 10 − 6 10^{-6} , 8 8 prompts × \times 8 8 completions per iteration, B = 17.0 B=17.0 M generated tokens, an evaluation every B / 16 B/16 , seeds 200 200 – 207 207 , and one A100 GPU per run (time axes are compared only within the study). Every configuration trains past B B , with budgets set from the time ratios of Table 13 so that its training time passes T c T_{c} , the training time that outcome-only GRPO needs to reach B B on the same seed. Outcome-only GRPO trains to 1.25 ​ B 1.25B , the k = 4 k=4 Monte-Carlo reward to 4 ​ B 4B , the k = 1 k=1 reward to 3 ​ B 3B , continuation reuse with k = 2 k=2 (every continuation is also trained on, as in Appendix E ) to 2 ​ B 2B , and the VinePPO-style value advantage with four clipped optimizer steps per batch to 3 ​ B 3B . The primary contrasts T1–T4 compare each configuration with outcome-only GRPO at T c T_{c} , with the configuration’s accuracy interpolated linearly in training time (paired t t over seeds, two-sided, Holm over the four). They were predicted to be negative for both Monte-Carlo rewards, with no predicted direction for the two variants with reuse. The two configurations with reuse adapt published methods to our trainer and recipe. Table 16 lists what each keeps and what it changes; the differences in branch points, samples per branch point, and update rule change both the variance of the credit and its cost. These results therefore describe the adaptations, not the published methods in their own settings. Table 16: What our dense-reward variants keep from the methods they adapt. Published settings are the main ones of each paper ( Kazemnejad et al., 2025 ; Guo et al., 2025 ; Hou et al., 2025 ) . Our configurations share one trainer and recipe (MATH L4–5, 8 8 prompts × \times 8 8 completions per iteration, Adam at a constant 10 − 6 10^{-6} , the strict reward in S35). The Monte-Carlo rewards ( k = 1 k=1 , 2 2 , 4 4 ) are our construction: segment credit from the same kind of continuations, with a fixed V ⁡ ( prompt ) = 0.5 V(\text{prompt})=0.5 , added to the group advantage, with one on-policy step. Figure 12: Strong local agreement can coexist with weak training results. Qwen2.5-1.5B, Monte-Carlo reward, equal iterations. (a) The three low-weight configurations satisfy the ± 0.03 \pm 0.03 equivalence criterion ( 14 14 paired seeds; thick and thin bars: 90 % 90\% and 95 % 95\% seed-bootstrap intervals). (b) High-weight configurations against their own matched outcome controls ( 95 % 95\% intervals); the α = 4 \alpha=4 deficit survives only the bootstrap Holm correction, and the starred configuration uses co-equal standardization, the others the max-normalized rule. (c) Four full-parameter probes with disjoint rollouts (mean projected reference cosine 0.84 0.84 ), measured separately from the training runs. Figure 13 traces the dense reward from the gradient probe to the training result. This experiment tests whether favorable local measurements pick out a useful dense reward in a short GRPO recipe. We fully fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct ( Qwen Team, 2024 ; Yang et al., 2024 ) on MATH problems of levels 4–5 ( Hendrycks et al., 2021 ) for 32 32 iterations, with 8 8 completions for each of 8 8 prompts per iteration. Separate probes, with LoRA and with full-parameter gradients, measure the gradient geometry on fixed batches. Training samples at temperature 1.0 1.0 with top- p p 0.95 0.95 and uses Adam with gradient clipping, whereas the reported accuracy uses greedy decoding. Greedy accuracy is therefore a different target from the expected accuracy J J under sampling used in the local analysis. The probes also compare against a reference gradient of progress estimated from a finite number of Monte-Carlo continuations, denoted h ^ MC \widehat{h}_{\mathrm{MC}} , rather than against the exact population gradient ( Sections H.1 and H.3 ). Terms. The Monte-Carlo reward assigns each of six segments of a completion the change in success rate across it, as estimated from k k continuations. In this study, the credit is centered within each completion (MC-within in the tables), normalized, and combined with the outcome advantage as A g + α ​ w g , h A_{g}+\alpha w_{g,h} . There are two normalizations: dividing by the credit’s largest absolute value in the batch (max-normalized, the default), or dividing by its standard deviation (co-equal standardization). The probes split the continuation samples into two halves: half A defines the progress reference h ^ MC \widehat{h}_{\mathrm{MC}} , and half B gives a candidate credit from disjoint samples. Gates act on the credit before centering. The sign-opposition gate keeps only the entries whose sign is opposite to A g A_{g} ; the rollout-disagreement gate weights each segment by p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) p(1-p) , where p p is the fraction of continuations that succeed from the segment’s end. The implicit reward is log ⁡ π θ − log ⁡ π ref \log\pi_{\theta}-\log\pi_{\mathrm{ref}} , the log ratio between the current policy and the reference policy. What the paired comparisons measure. For each seed, we compare the change in accuracy from start to end of the dense-reward configuration with that of the outcome-only configuration, using the same validation prompts and scoring procedure. Seed-bootstrap intervals describe uncertainty across seeds on these fixed prompts. Probes and training configurations are separate measurements, so Figure 12 sets them side by side rather than run by run. Matching seeds and training iterations also leaves a difference in compute, because the Monte-Carlo reward needs extra continuations from each prefix. Section 3.2 repeats the comparison at matched generated tokens; measuring the resources used by each stage is a separate validation step ( Sections H.1 and H.2 ). G.1 Does agreement with the reference survive disjoint sampling? The probe computes the summed scores of each completion, the outcome gradient, and the dense-reward gradient, and then projects the gradients onto V V ( Figure 15 ). The outcome gradient’s residual outside V V is 2 × 10 − 13 2{\times}10^{-13} in full-parameter space, a numerical check of the fixed-batch identity. For the MC-within reward, the strongest check uses four full-parameter probes with disjoint rollouts. Their alignments with an independently sampled half-A reference are 0.97 , 0.66 , 0.89 , 0.82 0.97,0.66,0.89,0.82 , with mean 0.84 0.84 . The larger values we also report, 0.95 0.95 in pooled LoRA probes and 0.96 0.96 in full-parameter probes, compare a credit computed from all rollouts with a reference that partly overlaps with it. Disjoint sampling removes the direct reuse of rollouts, but the reference still depends on the estimator’s finite horizon and segmentation. Does the credit add a useful direction? The Monte-Carlo reward, the implicit reward, and a permutation of the Monte-Carlo reward within each completion all produce updates that lie largely outside V V : the part outside V V carries 0.86 0.86 – 0.95 0.95 of the update’s norm ( Table 20 ). Only the Monte-Carlo reward consistently aligns with the estimated reference. The pooled alignments of the implicit-reward and shuffled rewards are − 0.01 ± 0.08 -0.01\pm 0.08 and − 0.01 ± 0.06 -0.01\pm 0.06 . Shuffling keeps the magnitudes of the token credits unchanged, but it can change the norm of the projected gradient and the Adam step. Interpolating between an independent half-B credit and noise also links the alignment of the projected components to the cosine between the full gradient and the reference ( Figure 14 ). Similar descriptive patterns appear across 8 checkpoints from five model families ( Table 21 ). These probes measure raw credits before the optimizer acts on them. The next question is whether this well-aligned reward improves training. G.2 Does the gradient test pick out a better training reward? The outcome-only baseline improves greedy validation accuracy by + 0.056 +0.056 [ + 0.041 , + 0.071 ] [+0.041,+0.071] ( Table 17 ). At α = 0.25 , 0.5 , 1 \alpha=0.25,0.5,1 , a prespecified expansion to n = 14 n=14 paired seeds supports equivalence with the baseline within ± 0.03 \pm 0.03 : the 90 % 90\% intervals lie inside this margin. The result holds with Holm correction over the three configurations, under percentile-bootstrap, paired- t t two one-sided (TOST), and studentized-bootstrap tests. Smaller benefits and harms remain unresolved. At α = 5 \alpha=5 , the paired deficit is − 0.124 -0.124 [ − 0.161 , − 0.087 ] [-0.161,-0.087] ( n = 8 n=8 , d z = − 2.15 d_{z}=-2.15 ), which survives Holm correction over the nine Monte-Carlo weight configurations of the primary harm analysis ( Section H.2 ) under both bootstrap and paired- t t tests. The deficit at α = 4 \alpha=4 survives only the bootstrap test. In this short training recipe, therefore, strong agreement with the reference coexists with equivalence at low weights and harm at high weights. The shuffled reward keeps token-credit magnitudes unchanged, but can change projected-gradient norms and Adam steps. Its α = 5 \alpha{=}5 comparison also changes the rule for combining credit with the outcome. The α = 4 \alpha{=}4 comparison in Table 22 uses the same rule. We next test choices made between estimating the reward and applying the optimizer. The coherent and shuffled rewards behave differently at high weight. Their paired difference-in-differences is − 0.128 -0.128 [ − 0.155 , − 0.098 ] [-0.155,-0.098] at α = 5 \alpha=5 , but those configurations use different rules for combining credit with the outcome. At α = 4 \alpha=4 , where the rules match, it is − 0.074 -0.074 [ − 0.111 , − 0.040 ] [-0.111,-0.040] over five common seeds ( Section H.5 ). These controls match the magnitudes of the token credits, but the magnitudes of the projected gradients and the actual Adam steps can still differ. Along two paired training runs, agreement with the reference remains high both in the low-weight configuration and in the degrading high-weight configuration (minimum 0.969 0.969 against the strict half-B reference; Figure 16 ). Agreement thus stays high while accuracy falls, which points to how the credit is combined and how the optimizer acts, rather than to a decay in alignment, as questions for further study. Can gating help? A prespecified expansion of the sign-opposition gate at α = 1 \alpha=1 gives + 0.008 +0.008 [ − 0.004 , + 0.021 ] [-0.004,+0.021] against its matched outcome-only control ( n = 32 n=32 , one-sided paired- t t p = 0.100 p=0.100 , d z = 0.23 d_{z}=0.23 ; Table 18 ). The expansion includes eight exploratory seeds, and its interval includes a gain of 2.1 2.1 percentage points. The smaller gate comparisons are descriptive. The sign-opposition configuration at α = 3 \alpha=3 gives + 0.036 +0.036 [ − 0.013 , + 0.080 ] [-0.013,+0.080] , on the same validation prompts and scoring procedure as its outcome-only control; no matched ungated comparison is available. Rollout-disagreement gating improves on the ungated reward with the same rule by + 0.056 +0.056 [ + 0.007 , + 0.094 ] [+0.007,+0.094] at α = 3 \alpha=3 ( n = 5 n=5 ), but the paired- t t test does not exclude zero ( p = 0.089 p=0.089 ). Masking changes the total amount of credit as well as where it is placed, and centering afterward can put credit back on the masked positions ( Section G.5 ). Finally, we check how far the pattern extends. The Qwen2.5-7B sweep has 43 43 dense runs, with 3 3 – 5 5 seeds per configuration. No dense-reward configuration’s mean gain exceeds that of the outcome-only baseline, which itself gains 0.21 0.21 , and the instability at high weights varies by seed ( Table 23 ). SmolLM3-3B ( n = 10 n=10 for the outcome-only configuration and the configurations at α = 0.5 \alpha=0.5 and 5 5 ) and a small NuminaMath study show similar point estimates at lower statistical resolution ( Tables 24 and 26 ). The single-seed Phi-4 pilots are positive, with paired gains of + 0.093 +0.093 and + 0.080 +0.080 at α = 0.5 \alpha=0.5 and 5 5 ( Section H.4 ). These positive pilots show why each recipe needs its own replication. Do the reward source and the rollout budget matter? With co-equal standardization, the Monte-Carlo reward also gives a small difference at low weight and a deficit at high weight ( n = 5 n=5 ; Table 22 ). An ablation of the implicit reward with a frozen reference shows a large loss in accuracy ( d z = − 7.81 d_{z}=-7.81 , n = 3 n=3 , descriptive). The implicit reward uses the current policy in its numerator, whereas PRIME uses a separately trained implicit PRM ( Cui et al., 2026 ) . A frozen PRM trained on step labels also fails to improve accuracy when converted to credit centered within each completion, but its segmentation differs from the PRM’s native setup and its scoring head was rebuilt, which limits this comparison ( Table 25 ). Finally, a 20 20 -iteration sweep over k ∈ { 2 , 6 , 16 } k\in\{2,6,16\} continuations at α = 1 \alpha=1 ( n = 4 n=4 ) detects no improvement at those budgets. Noise in the estimator remains a possible contributor, although in the matched-compute recipe continuation sampling contributes only 4 % 4\% of the second moment of the k = 4 k=4 update ( Section 3.2 ). In the primary short-horizon recipe, then, high agreement between the gradient and the reference did not by itself pick out a reward that beat the declared margin, which is why the matched-compute studies of Appendix C measure training outcomes directly. Figure 13: How a dense reward goes from its estimate to the training result. In the primary full-fine-tuning recipe, the Monte-Carlo reward is estimated, optionally gated, centered, normalized, and combined with outcome-only GRPO before global gradient-norm clipping and one Adam step per iteration. Gating comes before centering, which can put credit back on cleared tokens. The α = 5 \alpha=5 MC-within configuration uses co-equal standardization; the other primary weights and shuffled controls use the max-normalized rule. Training comparisons use the same seed, validation prompts, and scoring procedure. The diagnostic branch measures the fixed-batch score map and agreement with a finite-budget Monte-Carlo reference. The training branch measures same-seed greedy-accuracy differences after Adam updates ( Tables 19 and H.1 ). Compute. The equal-iteration experiments of Section 3.1 used a node with eight H100-80GB GPUs. A 12 12 -iteration probe on one GPU takes about 20 20 minutes, a 32 32 -iteration training run for the 1.5 1.5 B model takes 1 1 – 2 2 GPU-hours, and a 7 7 B run on two GPUs takes 4 4 – 6 6 GPU-hours. The 27 27 B/ 35 35 B Qwen3.6 and 20 20 B probes use two-GPU sharding for inference, and the equal-iteration study as a whole, with its ablations of weight, continuation budget, combination rule, and model family, used a few hundred GPU-hours. The matched-compute studies of Section 2.3 used one GPU per run (an H200 for all but 22 22 of about 1,320 1{,}320 GPU-hours) and report training time per run ( Appendix C ). A systems-performance evaluation would also need the runtime and memory of each stage. G.5 Gate protocols and comparisons Gates test whether concentrating the credit on the segments where it should matter most helps: the opposition gate keeps the credit that opposes the outcome advantage, and the disagreement gate favors segments whose continuations disagree. The Monte-Carlo gates use the Qwen2.5-1.5B full-fine-tuning recipe: 32 32 iterations, 8 8 prompts, group size 8 8 , learning rate 10 − 5 10^{-5} , no KL penalty, six segments, and k = 4 k=4 continuations. The opposition gate keeps the entries whose sign is opposite to A g A_{g} , and the disagreement gate weights each segment by p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) p(1-p) , where p p is the fraction of continuations that succeed. Both are applied before centering and normalization. We normalize the peak credit within each configuration, which does not match the total credit mass, the norm of the projected gradient, or the size of the actual optimizer step across configurations. Centering also changes which positions have nonzero credit. We trained no control that keeps the entries agreeing with the outcome’s sign while preserving the mask. Expanded opposition gate. The pilot at α = 1 \alpha=1 gave a paired mean of + 0.013 +0.013 ( d z = 0.35 d_{z}=0.35 ), with four of eight differences negative. We fixed the analysis plan before adding seeds and then analyzed the combined result, with n = 32 n=32 , once. All 64 64 dense and control runs reached iteration 31 31 . No gated run meets the collapse criterion, a drop of 0.15 0.15 from peak to final accuracy; the largest drop is 0.067 0.067 . The expansion includes the eight pilot seeds. Its post hoc power at the observed effect is 0.37 0.37 , and the interval remains compatible with an improvement of 2.1 2.1 points. Other opposition weights. They change the mask while keeping the recipe fixed, but they do not hold the size of the resulting gradient or optimizer step fixed. The implicit-reward configurations use a frozen policy reference; they are not PRIME replications. Training and evaluation. The main policy is Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct . LoRA probes train rank- 16 16 adapters (adapter scale 32 32 ) on query and value projections; the main training study updates all parameters. Each iteration samples eight completions for each of eight prompts, using temperature 1.0 1.0 , top- p p 0.95 0.95 , and at most 380 380 new tokens. Adam uses β 1 = 0.9 \beta_{1}=0.9 , β 2 = 0.999 \beta_{2}=0.999 , no weight decay, constant learning rate, global gradient-norm clipping at 1.0 1.0 , and bfloat16 weights. There is one on-policy gradient step per iteration, no inner-epoch loop, and no importance-ratio clipping. Dense weights stay constant throughout each run. The six-segment end-task credits use up to 200 200 continuation tokens per prefix. The verifier extracts the last number inside the final \boxed{} , then falls back to the last number after #### or the last number in the completion. It strips a <think> prefix and uses numerical tolerance 10 − 4 10^{-4} , or exact string matching otherwise. The same verifier scores continuations. Validation uses greedy decoding. The reported greedy accuracy and the expected accuracy under sampling therefore measure different quantities. Because of top- p p sampling, finite continuation length, fixed segmentation, and the initial success prior, the probe reference also differs from an exact population gradient. H.2 Statistical reporting Unless stated otherwise, end-task intervals are 95 % 95\% percentile-bootstrap intervals with 10 4 10^{4} seed resamples. A gain row resamples per-seed final-minus-start gains; a paired row resamples same-seed dense-minus-outcome differences. These intervals describe uncertainty across seeds on the fixed validation examples. The standardized paired effect is d z = Δ ¯ / s Δ d_{z}=\overline{\Delta}/s_{\Delta} , without a small-sample correction. Reported bootstrap p p values are twice the proportion of resampled means on the opposite side of zero. Because such p p values can overstate the evidence when n n is small, we give paired- t t sensitivity checks for the principal claims. Brackets denote intervals; ± \pm denotes the sample SD unless explicitly labeled SE. SD uses the n − 1 n-1 variance denominator; SE = SD / n \mathrm{SE}=\mathrm{SD}/\sqrt{n} . Small samples. At n = 3 n=3 , a 95 % 95\% percentile-bootstrap interval equals the observed minimum and maximum (an all-one-seed resample has probability 3 − 3 = 0.037 > 0.025 3^{-3}=0.037>0.025 ), so these rows describe the observed range. For the n = 6 n=6 , α = 3 \alpha=3 transition, exhaustive enumeration of all 6 6 = 46,656 6^{6}=46{,}656 resamples gives [ − 0.108 , + 0.001 ] [-0.108,+0.001] , and the paired- t t test gives t 5 = − 2.01 t_{5}=-2.01 , p = 0.101 p=0.101 , with five of six differences negative. For the other paired rows with n ≤ 7 n\leq 7 , exhaustive enumeration does not change whether the interval crosses zero. Probe rows pooled across checkpoints are correlated, so their spread is descriptive. Groups of comparisons and registration. The primary harm analysis includes nine Monte-Carlo weight configurations on the Qwen2.5-1.5B, 200 200 -problem, iteration- 31 31 recipe: five in Table 17 , two transition weights, and two co-equal-standardization configurations in Table 22 . The three low-weight equivalence tests form a separate Holm family. This group of comparisons excludes other models, datasets, evaluation sets, and the 20 20 -iteration estimator sweep; the source study has 31 31 Monte-Carlo configurations at iteration 31 31 ( 30 30 after merging two with the same configuration), 22 22 of them outside the nine-configuration family. Analysis plans were locked before adding data for the low-weight seed expansion, complementary-prover confirmation, SmolLM3 expansion, and n = 32 n=32 gate expansion. The primary low-weight expansion adds seeds 8 8 – 13 13 or 5 5 – 13 13 to existing configurations; the gate expansion retains its eight pilot seeds. Because the expansions add seeds to existing configurations, they extend those configurations rather than replicate them independently. Additional drift seeds. Two completed α = 5 \alpha=5 drift-probe seeds extend the paired estimate from − 0.124 -0.124 [ − 0.161 , − 0.087 ] [-0.161,-0.087] , d z = − 2.15 d_{z}=-2.15 , n = 8 n=8 , to − 0.123 -0.123 [ − 0.153 , − 0.093 ] [-0.153,-0.093] , d z = − 2.40 d_{z}=-2.40 , n = 10 n=10 . One eligible α = 0.5 \alpha=0.5 co-equal-standardization seed changes − 0.004 -0.004 [ − 0.032 , + 0.034 ] [-0.032,+0.034] to + 0.001 +0.001 [ − 0.026 , + 0.033 ] [-0.026,+0.033] ; its second drift run stops at iteration 20 20 and is excluded under the stated iteration- 28 28 admission rule. Drift seeds use their own outcome controls run at the same time. These runs serve as a sensitivity analysis; the nine-configuration family and the seed sets of the main tables are unchanged. Validation sets. The main 200 200 -problem MATH L3–5 set is used for the weight sweep, low-weight equivalence, PRM, implicit-reward, and disagreement-versus-ungated comparisons. A 150 150 -problem L4–5 set is used for opposition gates, prover, and cross-family configurations. A separate 115 115 -problem set is used only for the degenerate-slice ablation. Starting accuracies differ: Qwen2.5-1.5B/OLMo-2 means are 0.24 0.24 – 0.26 0.26 on the 200 200 -problem set and 0.15 0.15 – 0.20 0.20 on the 150 150 -problem set; SmolLM3 starts at 0.32 0.32 – 0.34 0.34 , Phi-4-mini at 0.25 0.25 , NuminaMath at 0.28 0.28 – 0.29 0.29 , and the degenerate slice at 0.03 0.03 – 0.10 0.10 . We therefore compare configurations only within an evaluation set. Table 18 uses the available opposition estimate from the same evaluation set and omits the unmatched ungated comparison. Deficits and collapse events. A negative paired configuration mean is different from a collapse within one run. We define a collapse event descriptively as final accuracy at least 0.15 0.15 below that run’s peak. We chose this threshold after inspecting variation in baseline drops. At 1.5 1.5 B, the Monte-Carlo α = 5 \alpha=5 and 4 4 configurations have 0 / 8 0/8 and 0 / 6 0/6 such events; their largest drops are 0.14 0.14 and 0.11 0.11 , versus 0.055 0.055 for outcome-only. Still, all eight and six seeds have negative absolute gains and paired differences. One of five α = 3 \alpha=3 co-equal-standardization seeds has a drop exactly 0.150 0.150 . One α = 5 \alpha=5 and two α = 4 \alpha=4 seeds peak at iteration 0 0 , so a rise-then-decay trajectory does not describe every deficit. Across 85 85 eligible gate/control runs ( 86 86 launched, one stopping at iteration 8 8 ), no drop reaches 0.15 0.15 ; the largest is 0.080 0.080 . Per-configuration bootstrap values. For Table 17 , the two-sided bootstrap p p values are 0.638 , 0.740 , 0.714 0.638,0.740,0.714 at α = 0.25 , 0.5 , 1 \alpha=0.25,0.5,1 , below 10 − 3 10^{-3} at 4 , 5 4,5 , and 0.304 0.304 for the shuffled control. For Table 22 , they are 0.014 , 0.060 0.014,0.060 for weights 2 , 3 2,3 ; 0.747 , 0.0004 0.747,0.0004 for co-equal-standardization weights 0.5 , 3 0.5,3 ; 0.134 , 0.390 , 0.248 0.134,0.390,0.248 for shuffled weights 1 , 3 , 4 1,3,4 ; and below 10 − 3 10^{-3} for the three-seed implicit-reward row. H.3 Gradient probes This subsection describes how the gradient tests of Section 2.4 are computed at language-model scale. Figure 15: Gradient-reference measurements at a fixed policy and batch. Weighted backward passes compute completion-score sums { q g } \{q_{g}\} , outcome gradient g out g_{\mathrm{out}} , dense gradients grad ⁡ ( w ) \operatorname{grad}(w) , and Monte-Carlo reference h ^ MC \widehat{h}_{\mathrm{MC}} in LoRA parameter space. Projecting onto V = span ⁡ { q g } V=\operatorname{span}\{q_{g}\} gives the confinement residual, projected alignment, and full-gradient reference cosine. The lower axis illustrates ⟨ P ⟂ ​ h ^ MC , P ⟂ ​ grad ⁡ ( w ) ⟩ \langle P_{\!\perp}\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{MC}},P_{\!\perp}\operatorname{grad}(w)\rangle : positive rescaling preserves its sign, while a direction change can change it. This inner product measures agreement with an estimated reference. The population directional derivative and actual Adam update are separate quantities. Score-gradient map. At a fixed checkpoint, we compute a credit gradient with one weighted backward pass: grad ( w ) = ∇ θ ∑ n , t w n , t 1 [ generated ] log π θ ( a n , t ) . \operatorname{grad}(w)=\nabla_{\theta}\sum_{n,t}w_{n,t}\,\mathbf{1}[\mathrm{generated}]\log\pi_{\theta}(a_{n,t}). We accumulate gradients over completion microbatches using logsumexp token probabilities. The probe builds V V from at most 24 24 completions and forms g out g_{\mathrm{out}} from the same subsample, so the least-squares projection uses this fixed, subsampled V V . It costs O ⁡ ( N ) O(N) forward passes, with the backward graphs kept in memory. The reported outcome residual 0.012 ± 0.008 0.012\pm 0.008 checks the numerical projection rather than the theorem itself; with full-parameter gradients, the residuals are as small as 2 × 10 − 13 2\times 10^{-13} . Progress reference and sample overlap. Following Monte-Carlo prefix-value methods ( Kazemnejad et al., 2025 ; Wang et al., 2024 ) , the probe uses three interior cuts and the endpoint, with up to 200 200 continuation tokens, temperature 1.0 1.0 , top- p p 0.95 0.95 , and initial success prior 0.5 0.5 . Each segment’s credit is the change in success rate across it. Half A of the continuations defines h ^ MC \widehat{h}_{\mathrm{MC}} , and half B, where labeled, provides a candidate credit from disjoint samples. The credit computed from all rollouts, which the primary pooled measurement uses, overlaps with half A. After projection, it differs from the centered full credit only by the projection residual (agreement within 1.4 × 10 − 3 1.4\times 10^{-3} over 135 135 rows), so the reported agreement with the reference, 0.95 0.95 , uses overlapping samples. The full-parameter alignments using disjoint samples are 0.97 , 0.66 , 0.89 , 0.82 0.97,0.66,0.89,0.82 , mean 0.84 0.84 . The overlapping full-parameter mean is 0.96 0.96 , with mean ± 1.96 \pm 1.96 SE interval [ 0.92 , 0.99 ] [0.92,0.99] (percentile bootstrap [ 0.92 , 0.98 ] [0.92,0.98] ), shuffled mean − 0.05 -0.05 , and off- V V fraction 0.93 0.93 . Controls and interpolation. Permuting values within each completion preserves the distribution of token-credit magnitudes. It does not, in general, preserve ‖ P ⟂ ​ S ⊤ ​ w ‖ \|P_{\!\perp}S^{\top}w\| , because the Gram matrix of the scores is not isotropic, and it need not preserve the actual Adam step. The interpolation family is w λ = λ ​ mc ^ B + ( 1 − λ ) ​ ξ w_{\lambda}=\lambda\widehat{\mathrm{mc}}_{B}+(1-\lambda)\xi , λ ∈ { 0 , 1 / 4 , 1 / 2 , 3 / 4 , 1 } \lambda\in\{0,1/4,1/2,3/4,1\} , with unit-normalized, zero-sum half-B and Gaussian rewards. The fractions of their updates that lie outside V V are similar, but their budgets are not exactly matched. The reported within-bin and magnitude-adjusted correlations are 0.88 ​ [ 0.84 , 0.92 ] 0.88\,[0.84,0.92] and 0.90 ​ [ 0.85 , 0.94 ] 0.90\,[0.85,0.94] ; the aligned-minus-shuffled reference-cosine gap is + 0.97 ​ [ + 0.93 , + 1.01 ] +0.97\,[+0.93,+1.01] . These measurements describe agreement with the measured reference at fixed probes. Implicit reward. This credit is w n , t = log ⁡ π θ ​ ( a n , t ) − log ⁡ π ref ​ ( a n , t ) w_{n,t}=\log\pi_{\theta}(a_{n,t})-\log\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{n,t}) , with the actor itself in the numerator and the initial policy in the denominator. We do not scale it in the probes, because the cosine and the fraction outside V V do not depend on scale. Averaged over each run, the cosines of the five runs stabilized by a KL penalty are − 0.05 , + 0.06 , + 0.01 , + 0.01 , + 0.10 -0.05,+0.06,+0.01,+0.01,+0.10 ; without the KL penalty, values at single checkpoints reach ± 0.77 \pm 0.77 , so alignment with the estimated reference can change sign. At initialization, the credit is zero because the actor and the reference are the same, so these summaries use only iterations after the first. This implicit reward differs from PRIME’s separate implicit PRM trained with outcome labels ( Cui et al., 2026 ) . H.4 End-task results and controls This subsection gives the end-task results behind Section 3.1 , with the checks behind each claim. Choice of recipe. A short LoRA recipe and a GSM8K recipe barely improved the outcome-only baseline, so they could not separate the rewards; the main study therefore uses full fine-tuning on MATH. Low-weight equivalence. At n = 14 n=14 paired seeds, the 90 % 90\% percentile-bootstrap intervals for α = 0.25 , 0.5 , 1 \alpha=0.25,0.5,1 are respectively [ − 0.023 , + 0.014 ] [-0.023,+0.014] , [ − 0.020 , + 0.013 ] [-0.020,+0.013] , and [ − 0.016 , + 0.010 ] [-0.016,+0.010] , with TOST p = 0.0095 , 0.0033 , 0.0004 p=0.0095,0.0033,0.0004 . Paired- t t TOST gives p = 0.025 , 0.012 , 0.003 p=0.025,0.012,0.003 , also passing Holm, and studentized-bootstrap 90 % 90\% intervals also lie inside the margin. The reported minimum effects detectable by superiority tests are 0.025 0.025 – 0.035 0.035 at 80 % 80\% power; estimated power at an effect equal to the margin ranges from 0.67 0.67 to 0.92 0.92 . These results support equivalence at the stated resolution. High-weight deficits. These controls check the most direct explanations: a decay of alignment during training, where the credit falls, the coherence of the credit, and changes in length and entropy. Alignment along training. Paired full-fine-tuning runs at α = 0.5 , 5 \alpha=0.5,5 , two seeds each, save checkpoints every four iterations. Full-parameter probes use G = 16 G=16 , k = 10 k=10 . The alignment between the full credit and the overlapping half-A reference stays at or above 0.992 0.992 (from 0.993 0.993 to 1.000 1.000 for seed 1 1 ). Length, entropy, and drift. On a fixed 100 100 -prompt subsample, response length stays within 1 % 1\% of the low-weight configuration through 28 28 iterations while high-weight accuracy moves from 0.25 0.25 to 0.12 0.12 ; the fraction of degenerate completions does not rise. High-weight entropy rises from 0.21 0.21 to 0.58 0.58 , versus 0.18 0.18 – 0.20 0.20 for low weight, and KL from the initial policy is about 30 30 times larger. These changes occur together and are consistent with several optimization explanations, such as rising entropy or over-optimization of the proxy reward. H.7 Degenerate-group channel ablation Groups whose completions all fail give outcome-only GRPO no signal. Dense rewards are often motivated as a way to learn from such groups, whereas prompt selection ( Section 4 ) avoids them, so we ask whether dense rewards help where they dominate. On a curated MATH slice, the fraction of groups with identical outcomes is 0.894 0.894 (per-iteration range 0.50 0.50 – 1.00 1.00 ). Qwen2.5-1.5B is full-fine-tuned for 32 32 iterations with group size eight, co-equal standardization, α = 1 \alpha=1 , and seeds 0 0 – 2 2 . The dense credit is zero-sum within each completion, which removes its completion-mean term but generally leaves a component in V V ; at language-model scale, the measured component in V V carries about 0.26 0.26 of the squared norm. Outcome gains are + 0.017 , + 0.009 , + 0.052 +0.017,+0.009,+0.052 (mean + 0.026 +0.026 ), versus − 0.070 , + 0.009 , + 0.009 -0.070,+0.009,+0.009 (mean − 0.017 -0.017 ) for the dense-reward configuration. The paired effect is − 0.043 -0.043 [ − 0.087 , 0.000 ] [-0.087,0.000] over three seeds (descriptive). All completions in the degenerate groups are wrong to the reported precision, and training accuracy is about 0.02 0.02 . Co-equal standardization amplifies a low-signal progress reward, and a frozen math-specialized prover succeeds on 13 % 13\% of 150 150 tested prompts with k = 4 k=4 , so progress is hard to estimate in this setting: where the outcome gradient is zero, a dense signal can exist without helping. Attributing the gains of other degenerate-group methods ( Palandye et al., 2026 ; Agrawal et al., 2026 ) to a particular channel would need a control that trains only the completion-mean term. It uses the same trainer for 32 32 iterations and seeds 0 0 – 2 2 ; the outcome baseline gains + 0.098 +0.098 ( Table 26 ). These rows use small samples and are descriptive. The positive single-seed Phi-4 pilots appear in Section H.4 . Appendix I What a Dense Reward Adds to One Update This section states the fixed-batch results behind the alignment test of Section 2.4 . They need only linear algebra and apply to any differentiable policy on a fixed batch. Proofs are in Appendix K , and Table 27 collects the notation. Notation. We use the notation of Section 2.1 , with completions of length H H and parameters θ ∈ ℝ P \theta\in\mathbb{R}^{P} . Stacking the token scores s g , h s_{g,h} into S ∈ ℝ G ​ H × P S\in\mathbb{R}^{GH\times P} , a token-level credit w w (one coefficient per sampled token) contributes grad ⁡ ( w ) = S ⊤ ​ vec ⁡ ( w ) , \operatorname{grad}(w)=S^{\top}\operatorname{vec}(w), (4) before token-specific clipping, importance weighting, KL terms, and optimizer state, which determine the effective update. Let B ∈ ℝ G × G ​ H B\in\mathbb{R}^{G\times GH} sum tokens within each completion (in this section and in Appendix K , B B denotes this matrix, not the token budget), K = S ​ S ⊤ K=SS^{\top} , V = colspace ⁡ ( S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ) = span ⁡ { q 1 , … , q G } V=\operatorname{colspace}(S^{\top}B^{\top})=\operatorname{span}\{q_{1},\dots,q_{G}\} , P V = Proj V P_{V}=\operatorname{Proj}_{V} , and P ⟂ = I − P V P_{\!\perp}=I-P_{V} . Any reweighting of whole completions updates within V V ; this is broader than the outcome baseline, whose centered coefficients span only S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ​ 𝟏 ⟂ S^{\top}B^{\top}\mathbf{1}^{\perp} . We write w = w traj + w within w=w_{\mathrm{traj}}+w_{\mathrm{within}} , where ( w traj ) g , h = H − 1 ​ ∑ j w g , j (w_{\mathrm{traj}})_{g,h}=H^{-1}\sum_{j}w_{g,j} and B ​ vec ⁡ ( w within ) = 0 B\operatorname{vec}(w_{\mathrm{within}})=0 , and J ⁡ ( θ ) = 𝔼 τ ∼ π θ ​ R ​ ( τ ) J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}}R(\tau) for the expected accuracy, with gradient h = ∇ J ​ ( θ ) h=\nabla J(\theta) . I.1 What can a dense reward add to one update? Outcome coefficients and completion means produce updates in V V ( Propositions 2 and 3 ). The next result gives the exact condition under which a token-level credit stays in this space, accounting for the score map’s kernel: different token credits can produce the same gradient. Theorem 1 (Sample-conditional equivalence) . For every credit w w , grad ( w ) ∈ V ⟺ vec ⁡ ( w ) ∈ colspace ⁡ ( B ⊤ ) + ker ⁡ ( S ⊤ ) . \begin{gathered}\operatorname{grad}(w)\in V\quad\Longleftrightarrow\\
\operatorname{vec}(w)\in\operatorname{colspace}(B^{\top})+\ker(S^{\top}).\end{gathered} Moreover, dim ( P ⟂ ​ S ⊤ ​ ker ⁡ B ) = rank ⁡ ( S ) − dim V \dim\bigl(P_{\!\perp}S^{\top}\ker B\bigr)=\operatorname{rank}(S)-\dim V . If rank ⁡ ( S ) = G ​ H \operatorname{rank}(S)=GH , equivalence holds exactly for completion-constant credits, and the residual dimension is G ⁡ ( H − 1 ) G(H-1) . Centering credit within each completion does not generally make its gradient orthogonal to V V ( Theorem 3 ). Once a credit adds a direction outside V V , we can ask whether that direction helps a local target. Proposition 1 (First-order projection identity) . For arbitrary h = ∇ J ​ ( θ ) h=\nabla J(\theta) and dense direction d = grad ⁡ ( w ) d=\operatorname{grad}(w) , the first-order advantage of d d over its matched projection P V ​ d P_{V}d is ⟨ h , d − P V ​ d ⟩ = ⟨ P ⟂ ​ h , P ⟂ ​ d ⟩ . \langle h,d-P_{V}d\rangle=\langle P_{\!\perp}h,P_{\!\perp}d\rangle. Only the components of d d and h h outside V V decide whether d d improves on its projection to first order. This is the alignment test of Section 2.4 , which Section 3.1 measures against a sampled reference. It compares a direction with its own projection at one policy, whereas an outcome-only training run follows its own sequence of policies and sampled batches. Appendix J Notation Table 27 collects the notation for the sampled score map, the population gradient, and the experiments. It does not guarantee a gain over a separate outcome-only training run. Appendix K Proofs and Supporting Geometry All vector spaces below are finite-dimensional and use the Euclidean inner product. We flatten credits before multiplying them by S ⊤ S^{\top} or B B . The results allow rank-deficient score matrices, including S = 0 S=0 . K.1 The sampled score map Proposition 2 (Decomposition) . For every w w , grad ⁡ ( w ) = grad ⁡ ( w traj ) + grad ⁡ ( w within ) \operatorname{grad}(w)=\operatorname{grad}(w_{\mathrm{traj}})+\operatorname{grad}(w_{\mathrm{within}}) and grad ⁡ ( w traj ) = ∑ g w ¯ g ​ q g ∈ V \operatorname{grad}(w_{\mathrm{traj}})=\sum_{g}\bar{w}_{g}q_{g}\in V . Proof. Apply the linear map S ⊤ S^{\top} to the decomposition of token coefficients. A completion-constant credit is B ⊤ ​ w ¯ B^{\top}\bar{w} , so its image is S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ​ w ¯ S^{\top}B^{\top}\bar{w} .
∎ Proposition 3 (Outcome confinement on the realized batch) . The set of score contributions with arbitrary real completion coefficients equals V V , and g out ∈ V g_{\mathrm{out}}\in V . Restricting coefficients to 𝟏 ⟂ \mathbf{1}^{\perp} gives S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ​ 𝟏 ⟂ S^{\top}B^{\top}\mathbf{1}^{\perp} , of dimension at most G − 1 G-1 . Proof. The unrestricted set is the image of Q = S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ Q=S^{\top}B^{\top} . Restricting its domain gives the second image and rank bound. The sampled rewards further constrain the normalized, zero-sum advantages. They select one update from this comparison space.
∎ Proof of Theorem 1 . The condition S ⊤ ​ w ∈ colspace ⁡ ( S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ) S^{\top}w\in\operatorname{colspace}(S^{\top}B^{\top}) holds if and only if there is a c c with S ⊤ ​ ( w − B ⊤ ​ c ) = 0 S^{\top}(w-B^{\top}c)=0 . This is exactly w ∈ colspace ⁡ ( B ⊤ ) + ker ⁡ ( S ⊤ ) w\in\operatorname{colspace}(B^{\top})+\ker(S^{\top}) . If S S has full row rank, its transpose is injective, so the kernel is zero. The dimension claim follows from the next result.
∎ Theorem 2 (Reachable residual dimension) . Let Y = S ⊤ ​ ker ⁡ B Y=S^{\top}\ker B and W = colspace ⁡ ( S ⊤ ) W=\operatorname{colspace}(S^{\top}) . Then W = V + Y W=V+Y and dim ( P ⟂ ​ Y ) = rank ⁡ ( S ) − dim V ≤ G ⁡ ( H − 1 ) . \dim(P_{\!\perp}Y)=\operatorname{rank}(S)-\dim V\leq G(H-1). Equality with the right side holds when rank ⁡ ( S ) = G ​ H \operatorname{rank}(S)=GH . Proof. The coefficient space decomposes as ℝ G ​ H = colspace ⁡ ( B ⊤ ) ⊕ ker ⁡ B \mathbb{R}^{GH}=\operatorname{colspace}(B^{\top})\oplus\ker B . Applying S ⊤ S^{\top} gives W = V + Y W=V+Y . On Y Y , projection onto V ⟂ V^{\perp} has kernel V ∩ Y V\cap Y . Therefore, dim ( P ⟂ ​ Y ) = dim Y − dim ( V ∩ Y ) = dim W − dim V \dim(P_{\!\perp}Y)=\dim Y-\dim(V\cap Y)=\dim W-\dim V .
It is at most dim ker ⁡ B = G ⁡ ( H − 1 ) \dim\ker B=G(H-1) . If S ⊤ S^{\top} is injective, dim W = G ​ H \dim W=GH and dim V = G \dim V=G . At S = 0 S=0 both sides of the exact identity are zero; at H = 1 H=1 the within space is zero.
∎ Theorem 3 (Projection through the score Gram matrix) . For B ​ ω = 0 B\omega=0 , the projection of S ⊤ ​ ω S^{\top}\omega onto V V is S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ ​ c S^{\top}B^{\top}c , where ( B ​ K ​ B ⊤ ) ​ c = B ​ K ​ ω . (BKB^{\top})c=BK\omega. (5) The Moore–Penrose solution gives the minimum-norm coefficient vector if the matrix is singular. If S ⊤ ​ ω ≠ 0 S^{\top}\omega\neq 0 , the fraction of the squared norm that lies in V V is c ⊤ ​ ( B ​ K ​ B ⊤ ) ​ c ω ⊤ ​ K ​ ω . \frac{c^{\top}(BKB^{\top})c}{\omega^{\top}K\omega}. Orthogonality holds exactly when B ​ K ​ ω = 0 BK\omega=0 . Proof. Least squares for Q = S ⊤ ​ B ⊤ Q=S^{\top}B^{\top} and d = S ⊤ ​ ω d=S^{\top}\omega gives Q ⊤ ​ Q ​ c = Q ⊤ ​ d Q^{\top}Qc=Q^{\top}d . These normal equations are consistent and give a unique projected vector even when c c is not unique. Substituting K = S ​ S ⊤ K=SS^{\top} gives equation 5 . The numerator is the squared norm of that vector, and the denominator is ‖ d ‖ 2 \|d\|^{2} . If d = 0 d=0 , the fraction is undefined. If K = λ ​ I K=\lambda I for λ > 0 \lambda>0 , every within-completion credit is orthogonal to V V ; positive overlap therefore does not hold in every case.
∎ Proof of Proposition 1 . The complementary orthogonal projectors are self-adjoint and satisfy P V ​ P ⟂ = 0 P_{V}P_{\!\perp}=0 . Thus ⟨ h , d ⟩ = ⟨ P V ​ h , P V ​ d ⟩ + ⟨ P ⟂ ​ h , P ⟂ ​ d ⟩ \langle h,d\rangle=\langle P_{V}h,P_{V}d\rangle+\langle P_{\!\perp}h,P_{\!\perp}d\rangle . Subtracting the rate of P V ​ d P_{V}d proves the identity. We do not need to represent h h through the finite sampled matrix. If we use an estimator h ^ \widehat{h} , the rate error satisfies | ⟨ P ⟂ ​ ( h ^ − h ) , P ⟂ ​ d ⟩ | ≤ ‖ h ^ − h ‖ ​ ‖ P ⟂ ​ d ‖ . \big|\langle P_{\!\perp}(\widehat{h}-h),P_{\!\perp}d\rangle\big|\leq\|\widehat{h}-h\|\,\|P_{\!\perp}d\|. An empirical alignment measurement alone does not bound this estimation error.
∎ K.1.1 Telescoping is not confinement Take one two-token completion with linearly independent scores s 1 , s 2 s_{1},s_{2} . The credit w = ( 1 , − 1 ) w=(1,-1) has zero total, yet s 1 − s 2 ∉ span ⁡ { s 1 + s 2 } = V s_{1}-s_{2}\notin\operatorname{span}\{s_{1}+s_{2}\}=V . It can represent the potential increments along a path with values 0 , 1 , 0 0,1,0 . Broadcasting the total instead produces the zero credit in V V . Potential-based shaping results concern particular reward, return and baseline constructions ( Ng et al., 1999 ) ; a telescoping total alone does not constrain the sampled score sum weighted by token coefficients. Likewise, when all group rewards agree, A = 0 A=0 and the outcome update is zero, but this does not guarantee that a nonzero dense update improves J J . Appendix L The Expected GRPO Update The results of Appendix I describe one sampled batch. This section gives the expected outcome-only GRPO update over fresh samples, which the matched-compute methods use to explain how adding a dense reward can change the relative weight of prompts ( Appendix C ). L.1 The population gradient is not confined to a sampled span Proposition 4 (Expected binary group-relative score) . Fix a prompt and a differentiable distribution over completions. Assume its support is independent of the parameters and that differentiation can pass under the expectation. Let R ⁡ ( τ ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } R(\tau)\in\{0,1\} be a fixed verifier, p ⁡ ( θ ) = 𝔼 ​ R ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p(\theta)=\mathbb{E}R\in(0,1) , G ≥ 2 G\geq 2 , and sample completions iid. Define q g = ∇ log ⁡ π θ ​ ( τ g ) q_{g}=\nabla\log\pi_{\theta}(\tau_{g}) , K R = ∑ g R g K_{R}=\sum_{g}R_{g} , and let s k s_{k} be the group reward standard deviation when K R = k K_{R}=k . With advantages equation 1 , 𝔼 [ 1 G ∑ g A g q g ] = λ G ( p ) ∇ p , λ G ​ ( p ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ K R ​ ( G − K R ) / ( G 2 ​ ( s K R + ϵ ) ) ] p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) > 0 . \begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g}A_{g}q_{g}\right]=\lambda_{G}(p)\nabla p,\\
\lambda_{G}(p)=\frac{\mathbb{E}[K_{R}(G-K_{R})/(G^{2}(s_{K_{R}}+\epsilon))]}{p(1-p)}>0.\end{gathered} (6) Groups with K R ∈ { 0 , G } K_{R}\in\{0,G\} contribute zero. Proof. The score identity gives 𝔼 ​ q = 0 \mathbb{E}q=0 and 𝔼 ⁡ [ R ​ q ] = ∇ p \mathbb{E}[Rq]=\nabla p , hence 𝔼 [ q ∣ R = 1 ] = ∇ p / p \mathbb{E}[q\mid R=1]=\nabla p/p and 𝔼 [ q ∣ R = 0 ] = − ∇ p / ( 1 − p ) \mathbb{E}[q\mid R=0]=-\nabla p/(1-p) .
Condition on K R = k K_{R}=k . By exchangeability, the expected sums of successful and failed completion scores are k ∇ p / p k\nabla p/p and − ( G − k ) ∇ p / ( 1 − p ) -(G-k)\nabla p/(1-p) . Their advantage coefficients are ( G − k ) / ( G ⁡ ( s k + ϵ ) ) (G-k)/(G(s_{k}+\epsilon)) and − k / ( G ( s k + ϵ ) ) -k/(G(s_{k}+\epsilon)) . Substituting these values and applying the outer 1 / G 1/G gives 𝔼 [ 1 G ∑ g A g q g | K R = k ] = k ⁡ ( G − k ) G 2 ​ ( s k + ϵ ) ​ p ​ ( 1 − p ) ∇ p . \mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g}A_{g}q_{g}\,\middle|\,K_{R}=k\right]=\frac{k(G-k)}{G^{2}(s_{k}+\epsilon)p(1-p)}\nabla p. Averaging proves the formula. Since p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) and G ≥ 2 G\geq 2 , a mixed group has positive probability. The multiplier is therefore positive. At p = 0 p=0 or p = 1 p=1 , all sampled advantages are zero and the displayed ratio is undefined.
∎ This score-function identity does not identify a new obstacle to population learning. Population GRPO analyses also study the normalization and success dynamics ( Mroueh, 2026 ) . Across prompts, λ G ​ ( p ) \lambda_{G}(p) can vary and reweight their gradients. Bias from clipping, off-policy sampling or other transformations needs a separate analysis. An update can belong to its sampled V V without its expectation belonging to any one realized V V . Experimental support, please view the build logs for errors. Generated by L A T E xml .
